 \le I\|u\|_2^2$$ for vector $u\in L^2(\mu)$ and independently chosen $|e_Q|\le1$. The vector extension follows by the same Parseval argument as in Lemma <a href="#lem:dyadic-test-bessel" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:dyadic-test-bessel">9</a>.

Fix a top cube $Q_0$ and a finite $\mathcal A\subset\mathcal F_1\cap\mathcal D(Q_0)$. Use, for every selected cube, the same set $S=B(z_{Q_0},(C_0+2)\ell(Q_0))$. The annular center and radius bounds ensure $B(a'_Q,2R_Q)\subset S$. Choose $e_Q$ from the annular vector as in Lemma <a href="#lem:annular-average-gap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:annular-average-gap">13</a>; then choose a single $$0<\varepsilon<\tfrac12\min_{Q\in\mathcal A}r_Q$$ when the family is nonempty. The average gap is greater than one for every selected $Q$ at this same truncation. Consequently $$\mu(A_Q)
 <\left|\int h_Q
       \langle e_Q,R_{\mu,\varepsilon}1_S\rangle\,d\mu\right|^2.$$ By <a href="#eq:haar-inner-parent-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:haar-inner-parent-mass">[eq:haar-inner-parent-mass]</a>, <a href="#eq:bounded-depth-haar-bessel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:bounded-depth-haar-bessel">[eq:bounded-depth-haar-bessel]</a>, and the operator bound, $$\sum_{Q\in\mathcal A}\mu(Q)
 \le H_I I C_{\rm R}^2\mu(S)
 \le H_I I C_{\rm R}^2
       \frac{G(C_0+2)^n}{c_1}\mu(Q_0).$$ The empty family is immediate. Taking the supremum over finite subfamilies proves that $\mathcal F_1$ is Carleson. Finally add the possible top level with $\ell(Q)>D_E$ to obtain $\mathcal F$. Its mass below any top cube is at most the mass of that top cube. The seed conclusion and its uniform depth bound hold for every cube outside this family. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:planar-limits"></a>
### Reflectionless limits and compatible normal coordinates

We first compare two planes using points near both of them. This gives one reference plane that controls the errors over a finite range of dyadic radii. We then identify the measure obtained when those errors vanish. Three assertions enter that identification separately: the limit is supported in a plane, its support fills the plane, and its density on that plane is constant.

<a id="pm-h-19"></a>
#### Comparison from measured errors

<a id="stmt-lem-plane-comparison"></a>
<div id="lem:plane-comparison" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:joint-plane-error"></a>
<a id="eq:plane-comparison"></a>
<a id="eq:distance-comparison"></a>
**Lemma 14** (Comparison of fitting planes). *Fix $1\le n\le d$, $G\ge0$, and $c>0$. There are constants $A>0$ and $0<\varepsilon_0\le1$, depending only on $n,d,G,c$, with the following property. Suppose that $\sigma$ has global upper $n$-growth $G$, $R>0$, and $\sigma(B(0,R))\ge cR^n$. If $S,W$ are affine $n$-planes and $0<\eta\le\varepsilon_0R$ satisfy $$\label{eq:joint-plane-error}
 \int_{B(0,R)}\bigl(\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(x,S)^2+\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(x,W)^2\bigr)\,d\sigma(x)
 \le \frac c2\eta^2R^n,$$ then $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(x,W)&\le A\eta(1+|x|/R) &&(x\in S),\label{eq:plane-comparison}\\
 |\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(x,S)-\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(x,W)|&\le A\eta(3+|x|/R)
       &&(x\in\mathbb R^d).\label{eq:distance-comparison}
\end{aligned}$$ The first conclusion also holds with $S$ and $W$ interchanged.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-plane-comparison"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-plane-comparison</summary>

*Proof.* Every integral in <a href="#eq:joint-plane-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:joint-plane-error">[eq:joint-plane-error]</a> is finite, since its integrand is bounded on the ball and upper growth implies local finiteness. Markov’s inequality shows that $$F=\{x\in B(0,R):\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(x,S)^2+\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(x,W)^2<\eta^2\}
 \quad\hbox{satisfies}\quad \sigma(F)\ge(c/2)R^n.$$ In particular, every point of $F$ is within $\eta$ of both planes.

<a id="eq:lower-dimensional-tube"></a>
Here is the quantitative nonconcentration needed to choose points in $F$. If $V$ is an affine $k$-plane, $k<n$, and $0<\tau\le1$, then $$\label{eq:lower-dimensional-tube}
 \sigma\{x\in\overline B(0,R):\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(x,V)<\tau R\}
 \le G2^n3^k\tau R^n.$$ Indeed, projection onto $V$, centered at the projection of $0$, sends $\overline B(0,R)$ into its intrinsic closed $R$-ball. A maximal $\tau R$-separated subset of this intrinsic ball has at most $(1+2/\tau)^k$ points: its disjoint open $\tau R/2$-balls lie in the $(R+\tau R/2)$-ball, and comparison of their $k$-dimensional volumes gives this bound. Maximality gives a $\tau R$-net. Ambient balls of radius $2\tau R$ about its points cover the indicated tube. Upper growth gives $G2^n3^k\tau^{n-k}R^n$, which implies <a href="#eq:lower-dimensional-tube" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lower-dimensional-tube">[eq:lower-dimensional-tube]</a>.

<a id="eq:anchor-conditioning"></a>
Choose $$\tau=\min\{1,c/[4(G2^n3^n+1)]\}.$$ The tube mass in <a href="#eq:lower-dimensional-tube" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lower-dimensional-tube">[eq:lower-dimensional-tube]</a> is strictly less than $(c/2)R^n$. Thus we can successively choose $p_0,\ldots,p_n\in F\cap\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\sigma$ such that, for $1\le j\le n$, $$\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}\bigl(p_j,p_0+\operatorname{span}
              \{p_i-p_0:1\le i<j\}\bigr)\ge\tau R.$$ At each step the affine span has dimension less than $n$, and $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\sigma$ has full measure. Put $v_j=p_j-p_0$. Then $|v_j|\le2R$. These vectors satisfy $$\label{eq:anchor-conditioning}
 \sum_{j=1}^n|t_j|\le \frac KR\left|\sum_{j=1}^nt_jv_j\right|,$$ where $K$ depends only on $n,\tau$. For completeness, define $K_0=0$ and $K_{j+1}=K_j(1+2/\tau)+1/\tau$. Projection perpendicular to the span of the first $j$ vectors bounds the last coefficient by the norm of the complete linear combination divided by $\tau R$. Subtracting its last term enlarges that norm by at most $1+2/\tau$. Induction proves <a href="#eq:anchor-conditioning" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:anchor-conditioning">[eq:anchor-conditioning]</a> with $K=K_n$. It also proves independence of the $v_j$.

Let $a=\pi_Sp_0$ and $w_j=\pi_Sp_j-a$, where $\pi_S$ is orthogonal projection. We have $|w_j-v_j|\le2\eta$. If $\eta/R\le1/[4(K+1)]$, <a href="#eq:anchor-conditioning" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:anchor-conditioning">[eq:anchor-conditioning]</a> and absorption give $$\sum|t_j|\le\frac{2K}{R}\left|\sum t_jw_j\right|.$$ The $n$ vectors $w_j$ consequently form a basis of the direction of $S$. Every projected anchor $a,a+w_1,\ldots,a+w_n$ lies within $2\eta$ of $W$. The orthogonal normal-coordinate map for $W$ is affine and has norm $\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(\cdot,W)$. Applying this affine map to $x=a+\sum t_jw_j$ gives $$\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(x,W)\le2\eta+4\eta\sum|t_j|
 \le2\eta+\frac{8K\eta}{R}|x-a|.$$ Since $|a|\le R+\eta\le2R$, this proves <a href="#eq:plane-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:plane-comparison">[eq:plane-comparison]</a>, for example with $A=2(1+8K)$ and $\varepsilon_0=\min\{1,1/[4(K+1)]\}$. All choices preceded $\sigma,R,S,W,\eta$.

Finally, for arbitrary $x$, let $p=\pi_Sx$. Since $|\pi_S0|\le|a|\le2R$ and the linear part of $\pi_S$ is a contraction, $|p|\le|x|+2R$. Hence $$\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(x,W)\le|x-p|+\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(p,W)
 \le\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(x,S)+A\eta(3+|x|/R).$$ Interchanging the planes proves <a href="#eq:distance-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:distance-comparison">[eq:distance-comparison]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-fixed-plane-moments"></a>
<div id="lem:fixed-plane-moments" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:fixed-plane-moments"></a>
**Lemma 15** (One reference plane through a finite horizon). *Fix $0<\gamma<1/2$ and the constants $n,d,G,c$ of Lemma <a href="#lem:plane-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:plane-comparison">14</a>. There are $\kappa>0$ and $M<\infty$, depending only on these data and $\gamma$, as follows. Suppose $K\ge0$ is an integer, $\delta>0$, $\delta2^{\gamma K}\le\kappa$, $\sigma$ has global upper growth $G$, and, for $0\le k\le K$, $$\sigma(B(0,2^k))\ge c2^{kn},
 \qquad e_\sigma(0,2^k)\le\delta2^{\gamma k}.$$ Then an affine $n$-plane $S$ satisfies $$\label{eq:fixed-plane-moments}
 \int_{B(0,2^k)}\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(x,S)^2\,d\sigma(x)
 \le M\delta^2 2^{k(n+2+2\gamma)}
 \qquad(0\le k\le K).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-fixed-plane-moments"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-fixed-plane-moments</summary>

*Proof.* Write $b=2^\gamma>1$ and $R_i=2^i$. The definition of the infimum in $e_\sigma$ and the positivity of $\delta b^i$ give affine planes $P_i$, $0\le i\le K$, with $$\int_{B(0,R_i)}\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(x,P_i)^2\,d\sigma
 \le2(\delta b^i)^2R_i^{n+2}.$$ No attainment of a least-squares infimum is required. For $0\le i<K$, restricting the error for $P_{i+1}$ to $B(0,R_i)$ shows that the sum of neighboring errors is at most $D(\delta b^i)^2R_i^{n+2}$, where $D=2+2\cdot2^{n+2}b^2$. Choose $L>0$ with $D\le L^2c/2$, and put $\eta_i=L\delta R_i b^i$. Taking $\kappa=\varepsilon_0/L$ ensures $\eta_i\le\varepsilon_0R_i$ through the horizon. Lemma <a href="#lem:plane-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:plane-comparison">14</a> gives, at the same arbitrary point $x$ in each step, $$\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(x,P_i)\le\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(x,P_{i+1})
       +AL\delta b^i(3R_i+|x|).$$ For $|x|\le R_k$, the sum of these errors for $i<k$ is bounded by $B\delta R_kb^k$, where $$B=AL\left(\frac3{2b-1}+\frac1{b-1}\right).$$ Indeed, sum the geometric series with ratios $2b$ and $b$ separately. Consequently $\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(x,P_0)\le\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(x,P_k)+B\delta R_kb^k$ on that ball. Squaring, integrating, and using upper growth proves <a href="#eq:fixed-plane-moments" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fixed-plane-moments">[eq:fixed-plane-moments]</a> with $S=P_0$ and $M=4+2GB^2$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-20"></a>
#### Rigidity on the limiting plane

We shall use the symmetric fractional operator <a id="eq:fractional-operator-definition"></a>$$\label{eq:fractional-operator-definition}
 \mathcal Lg(x)=\int_{\mathbb R^n}
 \frac{2g(x)-g(x+z)-g(x-z)}{2|z|^{n+1}}\,dz,
 \qquad g\in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R^n).$$ This integral is absolutely convergent: the numerator is $O(|z|^2)$ near zero and is bounded at infinity. Lemma <a href="#lem:fractional-rigidity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:fractional-rigidity">21</a> proves that a measurable function $v:\mathbb R^n\to\mathbb R$ satisfying $\int|v(x)|(1+|x|)^{-n-2}\,dx<\infty$ and $\int v\mathcal Lg=0$ for every compact smooth mean-zero test is affine almost everywhere. If $v$ is essentially bounded, it is constant almost everywhere. That lemma concerns functions on $\mathbb R^n$ and is proved independently of the limiting measures constructed here.

<a id="stmt-lem-planar-rigidity"></a>
<div id="lem:planar-rigidity" class="lemma">

**Lemma 16** (Rigidity of a planar reflectionless measure). *Let $1\le n\le d$, let $L$ be an affine $n$-plane, and let $\sigma$ be a nonzero Radon measure on $\mathbb R^d$ supported in $L$. Suppose that $c,G>0$ and $$\sigma(B(x,r))\le Gr^n\quad(x\in\mathbb R^d,\ r>0),
 \qquad
 \sigma(B(x,r))\ge cr^n\quad(x\in\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\sigma,\ r>0).$$ Suppose also that $\mathcal R_\sigma(\varphi)=0$ for every compactly supported Lipschitz function $\varphi$ with $\int\varphi\,d\sigma=0$. Assume the hard Riesz truncations are uniformly bounded on $L^2(\sigma)$. Then $$\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\sigma=L,
 \qquad \sigma=\theta\,\mathcal H^n\!\restriction L
 \quad\text{for some }\theta>0.$$ The same conclusion holds if the operator assumption on $\sigma$ is replaced by the following source assumption: $\sigma_j$ are Radon measures on $\mathbb R^d$ converging to $\sigma$ against compact continuous tests, with a common global upper $n$-growth bound and a common uniform hard-truncation $L^2$ bound. With the Euclidean normalization of $\mathcal H^n$, the resulting constant satisfies $c/\omega_n\le\theta\le G/\omega_n$, where $\omega_n=|B_{\mathbb R^n}(0,1)|$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-planar-rigidity"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-planar-rigidity</summary>

*Proof.* An affine isometry identifies $L$ with $\mathbb R^n$. Under this identification write $\mu$ for $\sigma$. The kernel and its scalar projections commute with this isometry. Reflectionlessness passes to $\mu$ with its actual measure: extend an intrinsic compact Lipschitz test by composing with orthogonal projection and multiplying by an ambient compact cutoff equal to one on its trace support. Its integral is unchanged. Choose a localization ball containing the extension and use the localization independence of the mean-zero pairing from Lemma <a href="#lem:pairings" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:pairings">5</a>. Thus no density or full-support assertion is needed to make this identification.

<a id="eq:planar-radius-difference"></a>
*Full support.* We first derive an annular bound using only growth and reflectionlessness. For a test supported in $\overline B(a,R/2)$, denote by $P_{a,R}(\varphi)$ the vector expression $$\begin{aligned}
 &\frac12\iint_{B(a,R)^2}
 K_n(x-y)\bigl(\varphi(x)-\varphi(y)\bigr)\,d\mu(x)d\mu(y)\\
 &\qquad+\int_{B(a,R)}\varphi(x)
 \int_{B(a,R)^c}\bigl[K_n(x-y)-K_n(a-y)\bigr]\,d\mu(y)d\mu(x).
\end{aligned}$$ Both terms are absolutely integrable by the Lipschitz cancellation near the diagonal and the extra power of decay in the exterior difference. This expression is defined without a mean-zero assumption; on mean-zero tests it equals $\mathcal R_\mu$. Splitting the larger ball into the smaller ball and its complement, and using oddness, gives $$\label{eq:planar-radius-difference}
 P_{a,R}(\varphi)-P_{a,r}(\varphi)
 =\left(\int\varphi\,d\mu\right)
   \int_{B(a,R)\setminus B(a,r)}K_n(a-y)\,d\mu(y)$$ when $0<r\le R$ and $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\varphi\subset\overline B(a,r/2)$. One may first perform the splitting with a finite outer cutoff; the integrable exterior differences justify its removal.

Choose a nonnegative Lipschitz bump equal to one on $B(a,s)$ and zero outside $B(a,2s)$, and divide it by its $\mu$-integral. For $a\in\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\mu$ the resulting function $\eta_s$ has integral one and satisfies $$\|\eta_s\|_\infty\le (cs^n)^{-1},\qquad
 \mathop{\mathrm{Lip}}(\eta_s)\le (cs^{n+1})^{-1},\qquad
 |P_{a,4s}(\eta_s)|\le C_n(G^2/c+G)=:A.$$ For the last estimate the interior term is at most a constant times $(cs^{n+1})^{-1}(Gs^n)(Gs)$; the exterior term is at most a constant times $Gs/s$, since $\int\eta_s\,d\mu=1$. Apply reflectionlessness to $\eta_{r/4}-\eta_{R/4}$ and then use <a href="#eq:planar-radius-difference" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:planar-radius-difference">[eq:planar-radius-difference]</a>. It follows that $$\left|\int_{B(a,R)\setminus B(a,r)}K_n(a-y)\,d\mu(y)\right|
 \le 2A\qquad(0<r\le R).$$ Letting inner radii decrease strictly to $\varepsilon$ gives the same bound on $\{y:\varepsilon<|y-a|<R\}$. At fixed $\varepsilon>0$ the kernel is integrable there, so this passage does not require zero mass on spheres.

Suppose now that $z\notin\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\mu$, and let $a$ be a nearest support point to $z$. Translate $a$ to zero and put $e=a-z\ne0$. The nearest-point property gives $$2\langle e,x\rangle+|x|^2\ge0\qquad(x\in\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\mu).$$ Define $p_e(x)=\max(0,\langle e,x\rangle)/|x|^{n+1}$ for $x\ne0$, and set $p_e(0)=0$. Then $$p_{-e}(x)\le\tfrac12|x|^{1-n},\qquad
 p_e(x)=\langle e,K_n(x)\rangle+p_{-e}(x).$$ Upper growth makes the first majorant integrable on bounded balls: its integral on $B(0,R)$ is at most $C_nGR$, as follows by dyadic annuli. The signed annular bound therefore bounds the nonnegative truncated integrals of $p_e$ on $B(0,1)$ uniformly. Monotone convergence proves $p_e\in L^1(\mu|_{B(0,1)})$.

Take a weakly convergent subsequence of the normalized blowups $\mu_r(A)=r^{-n}\mu(rA)$ as $r\downarrow0$. Upper growth supplies compactness, and the lower bound at zero makes the limit $\tau$ nonzero. These are the conclusions of Lemma <a href="#lem:weak-compactness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:weak-compactness">6</a>, applied to the blowups. The rescaled quadratic inequality is $2\langle e,x\rangle+r|x|^2\ge0$; hence $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\tau\subset\{\langle e,x\rangle\ge0\}$. For every fixed $T>0$, $$\int_{B(0,T)}\max(0,\langle e,x\rangle)\,d\mu_r(x)
 \le T^{n+1}\int_{B(0,rT)}p_e(y)\,d\mu(y)\longrightarrow0.$$ Testing with compact nonnegative cutoffs shows that $\tau$ is also supported in $\{\langle e,x\rangle\le0\}$. It is consequently supported on a proper hyperplane in $\mathbb R^n$. This is impossible for a nonzero measure with upper $n$-growth: covering open sets by disjoint dyadic cubes and enclosing each cube in a ball shows that such a measure is bounded above by $C_nG'\,dx$, where $G'$ is its growth constant. A proper hyperplane has Lebesgue measure zero. This contradiction proves $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\mu=\mathbb R^n$.

<a id="eq:planar-two-sided-density"></a>
*A positive bounded density.* The same cube comparison gives $\mu=f\,dx$ with $f\le C_nG$ almost everywhere. Full support makes the lower ball bound valid at every point. Lebesgue differentiation then gives $f\ge c/\omega_n$ almost everywhere. Choose a measurable representative with $$\label{eq:planar-two-sided-density}
 0<c_1\le f(x)\le C_1<\infty\qquad(x\in\mathbb R^n).$$ Changing $f$ on a null set has not changed $\mu$.

<a id="eq:planar-local-cap-bound"></a>
*A local representative for the pairing.* Fix a unit direction $u\in\mathbb R^n$, a center $a$, and radii $0<H$ and $2H\le R$. Put $B=B(a,R)$ and $$T_{\varepsilon,B}1(x)=\int_B
 \frac{\langle u,x-y\rangle}{\max(\varepsilon,|x-y|)^{n+1}}\,d\mu(y).$$ There is a constant $D_1$, independent of $B$ and $\varepsilon>0$, such that $$\label{eq:planar-local-cap-bound}
 \|T_{\varepsilon,B}1\|_{L^2(\mu|_B)}
 \le D_1\mu(B)^{1/2}.$$ For the direct operator hypothesis, subtract the hard truncation. The error kernel is bounded by $\varepsilon^{-n}\mathbf1_{\{|x-y|\le\varepsilon\}}$. Its row and column integrals are at most $2^nG$, so Schur’s inequality gives <a href="#eq:planar-local-cap-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:planar-local-cap-bound">[eq:planar-local-cap-bound]</a> from the original operator bound. Restricting the hard operator to a ball preserves that bound, by extending the input by zero.

Here is also the justification under the source assumption. Work first in $\mathbb R^d$ on the ambient ball corresponding to $B$. Its boundary has $\sigma$-measure zero by <a href="#eq:planar-two-sided-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:planar-two-sided-density">[eq:planar-two-sided-density]</a>. Restricted source measures therefore converge weakly as finite measures. The same Schur estimate gives uniformly bounded capped bilinear pairings on every source restriction. For fixed $\varepsilon>0$ these pairings pass to the limit against two bounded continuous tests: the capped kernel is bounded continuous, as are the squared tests giving their $L^2$ norms. Take the input test to be one and the other test to be the capped output for the limiting finite measure. That output is bounded continuous, by dominated convergence at this fixed cap. The resulting bilinear estimate gives its genuine $L^2$ bound <a href="#eq:planar-local-cap-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:planar-local-cap-bound">[eq:planar-local-cap-bound]</a>. Isometric pullback gives precisely the displayed intrinsic transform. This argument passes no singular kernel through weak convergence.

Choose a subsequence $\varepsilon_j\downarrow0$ along which $T_{\varepsilon_j,B}1$ converges weakly in $L^2(\mu|_B)$ to $v$. This one subsequence works against every test in that Hilbert space. Oddness gives, for bounded Lipschitz $\varphi$, $$\int_B T_{\varepsilon,B}1\,\varphi\,d\mu
 =\frac12\iint_{B^2}
 \frac{\langle u,x-y\rangle(\varphi(x)-\varphi(y))}
      {\max(\varepsilon,|x-y|)^{n+1}}\,d\mu(x)d\mu(y).$$ Removing the cap on the right changes the integral by at most $C_nG\mathop{\mathrm{Lip}}(\varphi)\mu(B)\varepsilon$. Thus $v$ represents the actual interior singular pairing. On $B(a,H)$ the function $$F(x)=\int_{B(a,R)^c}
 \langle u,K_n(x-y)-K_n(a-y)\rangle\,d\mu(y)$$ is well defined and bounded: the kernel difference is at most $C_n|x-a||y-a|^{-n-1}$, whose exterior integral is at most $C_nG|x-a|/R$. Fubini now identifies the scalar component of $\mathcal R_\mu(\varphi)$ with $\int(v+F)\varphi\,d\mu$ for mean-zero compact Lipschitz tests supported in $B(a,H)$.

<a id="eq:planar-local-constant"></a>
Choose a compact Lipschitz test $\eta$ supported in $B(a,H)$ with $\int\eta\,d\mu=1$, using the positive mass of that ball, and put $b=\int(v+F)\eta\,d\mu$. For any smooth compact test $q$ in this smaller ball, reflectionlessness applied to $q-(\int q\,d\mu)\eta$ gives $\int(v+F-b)q\,d\mu=0$. Since $\mu=f\,dx$, the fundamental lemma for locally integrable functions applied to $(v+F-b)f$ proves $$\label{eq:planar-local-constant}
 v+F=b\quad\text{$\mu$-almost everywhere on }B(a,H).$$ This is a statement about the transform; constancy of $f$ has not yet been used or proved.

<a id="eq:planar-unweighted-local"></a>
*An unweighted equation and density constancy.* Let $g\in C_c^\infty(B(a,H))$ be real with $\int g\,dx=0$. By <a href="#eq:planar-two-sided-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:planar-two-sided-density">[eq:planar-two-sided-density]</a>, $g/f$ belongs to $L^2(\mu|_B)$. It is therefore an allowed test for the weak convergence, even though it need not be Lipschitz. Moreover, $\mu$ and Lebesgue measure have the same null sets. Testing with $g/f$ and using <a href="#eq:planar-local-constant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:planar-local-constant">[eq:planar-local-constant]</a> yields $$\label{eq:planar-unweighted-local}
 \lim_j\left[\int g(x)T_{\varepsilon_j,B}1(x)\,dx
                   +\int g(x)F(x)\,dx\right]=0.$$ The subsequence and $b$ were selected before $g$.

For a compact smooth function $g$, define the scalar test transform $$R_ug(x)=-\frac12\int_{\mathbb R^n}
 \frac{\langle u,h\rangle[g(x+h)-g(x-h)]}{|h|^{n+1}}\,dh.$$ The integral is absolutely convergent: the first difference is $O(|h|)$ near zero, and the translated compact supports control infinity. The analogous capped expression equals the capped transform of $g$ by translation and reflection. It converges pointwise to $R_ug$ and is uniformly bounded on bounded $x$-sets. If $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}g\subset B(a,H)$ and $\int g=0$, then for $|x-a|\ge2H$ and $\varepsilon\le H$ the cap is inactive and mean-zero cancellation gives $$|R_{u,\varepsilon}^{\rm cap}g(x)|
 \le C_n H\|g\|_1|x-a|^{-n-1}.$$ Together these bounds form an integrable majorant independent of small $\varepsilon$. Since $f$ is bounded, dominated convergence applies to $\int f R_{u,\varepsilon}^{\rm cap}g$.

<a id="eq:planar-density-riesz"></a>
For $\varepsilon\le H$, oddness and Fubini identify the expression in brackets in <a href="#eq:planar-unweighted-local" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:planar-unweighted-local">[eq:planar-unweighted-local]</a> with $-\int f R_{u,\varepsilon}^{\rm cap}g\,dx$. The local capped product is absolutely integrable. For the exterior term, use the centered kernel difference, which is absolutely integrable against $|g(x)|\,dx\,d\mu(y)$; cancel the center term using $\int g=0$ inside the $x$-integral before integrating over the infinite exterior. The open ball and its closed exterior are exact complements. Thus no separate divergent exterior integral or omitted boundary term occurs. It follows that $$\label{eq:planar-density-riesz}
 \int_{\mathbb R^n}f(x)R_ug(x)\,dx=0
 \qquad\left(g\in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R^n),\ \int g=0\right).$$ The ball may be chosen after the test, so this statement has no fixed support restriction.

<a id="eq:planar-riesz-gradient"></a>
Let $u_1,\ldots,u_n$ be an orthonormal basis and $\psi\in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R^n)$ be real. Each $g_i=\partial_{u_i}\psi$ has integral zero. We claim $$\label{eq:planar-riesz-gradient}
 \sum_{i=1}^nR_{u_i}(\partial_{u_i}\psi)(x)=\mathcal L\psi(x).$$ Indeed, set $$Q_x(t)=\int\frac{\psi(x+th)+\psi(x-th)-2\psi(x)}{|h|^{n+1}}\,dh.$$ Scaling gives $Q_x(t)=tQ_x(1)$ for $t>0$. Differentiation under the integral at $t=1$ is justified by second-derivative cancellation near zero; for $1/2\le t\le2$ the derivative integrand vanishes for all sufficiently large $|h|$ at fixed $x$. Consequently $$Q_x(1)=\int\frac{D\psi(x+h)h-D\psi(x-h)h}{|h|^{n+1}}\,dh.$$ Expand $h$ in the orthonormal basis and use the definition of $R_u$ to obtain <a href="#eq:planar-riesz-gradient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:planar-riesz-gradient">[eq:planar-riesz-gradient]</a>. Every pairing with bounded $f$ is integrable by the preceding estimates. Summing <a href="#eq:planar-density-riesz" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:planar-density-riesz">[eq:planar-density-riesz]</a> therefore gives $$\int f\mathcal L\psi\,dx=0\qquad(\psi\in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R^n)).$$ Real and imaginary parts give the same identity for complex tests. Boundedness supplies $\int|f(x)|(1+|x|)^{-n-2}\,dx<\infty$. Lemma <a href="#lem:fractional-rigidity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:fractional-rigidity">21</a> now makes $f$ affine almost everywhere. A bounded affine function is constant: any nonzero linear part is unbounded along a line, and continuity promotes an almost-everywhere bound to an everywhere bound. Thus $f=\theta$ almost everywhere, with $\theta>0$ by <a href="#eq:planar-two-sided-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:planar-two-sided-density">[eq:planar-two-sided-density]</a>. The original ball bounds give $c\le\theta\omega_n\le G$, completing the proof. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
#### A common flat limit and its normal coordinates

The passage from vanishing mean-zero pairings to a reflectionless weak limit is part of the compactness method of Jaye and Nazarov (Jaye and Nazarov 2018) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[JayeNazarov2018I](#ref-JayeNazarov2018I)]. Here the common growth bounds control the singular diagonal and exterior tails, as proved in Lemma <a href="#lem:weak-compactness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:weak-compactness">6</a>. We combine that local argument with the measured plane errors; we do not invoke a classification of arbitrary higher-codimension reflectionless measures.

<a id="stmt-prop-flat-limit"></a>
<div id="prop:flat-limit" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:flat-limit-source-oscillation"></a>
<a id="eq:flat-limit-measure"></a>
**Proposition 17** (The flat limit). *Fix $1\le n<d$, $c>0$, $0<G<\infty$, $0\le D_0<\infty$, and $0<\gamma<1/2$. Let $\mu_j$ be Radon measures on $\mathbb R^d$ with $0\in E_j=\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\mu_j$, global upper $n$-growth $G$, and $$\mu_j(B(x,r))\ge cr^n
 \quad(x\in E_j,\ 0<r\le\mathop{\mathrm{diam}}E_j).$$ Assume every hard-truncated Riesz transform on $L^2(\mu_j)$ has norm at most $D_0$. Let $\delta_j>0$, integers $K_j\ge0$, and $A_j,v_j>0$ satisfy <a id="eq:flat-limit-horizons"></a>$$\label{eq:flat-limit-horizons}
 \begin{gathered}
 \delta_j\longrightarrow0,\quad K_j\longrightarrow\infty,\quad
 \delta_j2^{\gamma K_j}\longrightarrow0,\\
 2^{K_j}\le\mathop{\mathrm{diam}}E_j,\quad A_j\longrightarrow\infty,\quad v_j\longrightarrow0.
 \end{gathered}$$ Suppose <a id="eq:flat-limit-source-excess"></a>$$\label{eq:flat-limit-source-excess}
 e_{\mu_j}(0,2^k)\le\delta_j2^{\gamma k}
 \qquad(0\le k\le K_j)$$ and $$\label{eq:flat-limit-source-oscillation}
 |\mathcal R_{\mu_j}(\varphi)|\le v_j
 \quad\text{if }\ \mathop{\mathrm{Lip}}\varphi\le1,\quad
 \mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\varphi\Subset B(0,A_j),\quad\int\varphi\,d\mu_j=0.$$ There is a subsequence, relabeled by $j$, with the following properties. All the input sequences are restricted along this same subsequence when we relabel them. For each index there are an affine plane $S_j=a_j+L_j(\mathbb R^n)$, tangent and normal linear isometries $$L_j:\mathbb R^n\longrightarrow\mathbb R^d,
 \qquad N_j:\mathbb R^{d-n}\longrightarrow\mathbb R^d,
 \qquad L_jL_j^*+N_jN_j^*=I,$$ such that $a_j$ is the point of $S_j$ nearest $0$, $a_j\to0$, and $L_j\to L_\infty$ in operator norm, where $L_\infty$ is an isometry. Along this same subsequence, $$\label{eq:flat-limit-measure}
 \mu_j\rightharpoonup q(L_\infty)_\#\mathcal L^n,
 \qquad \frac{c}{4^n\omega_n}\le q\le\frac{2^nG}{\omega_n},
 \qquad \omega_n=\mathcal L^n(B(0,1)).$$ Here convergence is against continuous compactly supported tests, and $\mathcal L^n$ in <a href="#eq:flat-limit-measure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:flat-limit-measure">[eq:flat-limit-measure]</a> denotes Lebesgue measure. The planes obey <a href="#eq:fixed-plane-moments" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fixed-plane-moments">[eq:fixed-plane-moments]</a> with a single constant $M=M(n,d,c,G,\gamma)$. In particular, writing $e_i$ for the standard basis of $\mathbb R^{d-n}$ and <a id="eq:flat-limit-normal-coordinate"></a>$$\label{eq:flat-limit-normal-coordinate}
 u_{j,i}(x)=\langle N_je_i,x-a_j\rangle,$$ we have <a id="eq:flat-limit-normal-moments"></a>$$\label{eq:flat-limit-normal-moments}
 \int_{B(0,2^k)}|u_{j,i}|^2\,d\mu_j
 \le M\delta_j^2 2^{k(n+2+2\gamma)}
 \qquad(0\le k\le K_j, 1\le i\le d-n).$$ Moreover, on every bounded ball, the supports converge bilaterally to $L_\infty(\mathbb R^n)$: each source support point approaches that plane uniformly, and each point of the plane approaches $E_j$ uniformly.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-flat-limit"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-flat-limit</summary>

*Proof.* After discarding finitely many indices, the smallness threshold in Lemma <a href="#lem:fixed-plane-moments" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:fixed-plane-moments">15</a> holds. That lemma gives $S_j$ and the uniform moment bounds. Each fixed-radius squared-distance integral tends to zero: choose a larger fixed dyadic radius first, and then use $K_j\to\infty$ and $\delta_j\to0$.

<a id="eq:moving-support-tube"></a>
This integral conclusion also controls all support points in bounded balls. If $x_j\in E_j\cap B(0,R)$ and $\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(x_j,S_j)\ge t>0$, the $1$-Lipschitz distance function is at least $t/2$ on $B(x_j,\min\{1,t/2\})$. This ball lies in $B(0,R+1)$, and its radius is admissible for all large $j$. The lower growth bound therefore forces a fixed positive lower bound on the squared-distance integral there, a contradiction. Thus $$\label{eq:moving-support-tube}
 \sup_{x\in E_j\cap B(0,R)}\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(x,S_j)\longrightarrow0
 \quad\text{for every fixed }R.$$ Because $0\in E_j$, the nearest points $a_j$ tend to $0$. Choose orthonormal tangent and complementary normal frames $L_j,N_j$. The compactness of the finite-dimensional set of tangent isometries gives a subsequence with $L_j\to L_\infty$; norm preservation shows that the limit is still an isometry. The normal frames are retained along this subsequence; their convergence is not required.

<a id="eq:flat-limit-lower-growth"></a>
Lemma <a href="#lem:weak-compactness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:weak-compactness">6</a>, followed if necessary by a further subsequence, gives a nonzero limit $\nu$ with $0\in\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\nu$, hard truncation bound $D_0$, global upper growth $2^nG$, and $$\label{eq:flat-limit-lower-growth}
 \nu(B(x,r))\ge4^{-n}cr^n
 \qquad(x\in\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\nu, r>0).$$ All frames and moment bounds are kept along the composition of the two subsequences. If $x\in\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\nu$, positivity of compact tests in every neighborhood of $x$ supplies nearby points of $E_j$ for all large $j$. Together with <a href="#eq:moving-support-tube" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:moving-support-tube">[eq:moving-support-tube]</a>, this gives $\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(x,S_j)\to0$. On the other hand the affine projections $$a_j+L_jL_j^*(x-a_j)\longrightarrow L_\infty L_\infty^*x.$$ It follows that $x=L_\infty L_\infty^*x$. At this stage we have proved only $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\nu\subset L_\infty(\mathbb R^n)$.

We next verify that this same $\nu$ is reflectionless. Let $\varphi$ be a compactly supported Lipschitz function with $\int\varphi\,d\nu=0$. The pairing-convergence assertion of Lemma <a href="#lem:weak-compactness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:weak-compactness">6</a> provides compact Lipschitz tests $\varphi_j$ of zero $\mu_j$-mean, with uniformly bounded supports and Lipschitz constants, such that $$\mathcal R_{\mu_j}(\varphi_j)\longrightarrow
 \mathcal R_\nu(\varphi).$$ Choose $C\ge1$ bounding their Lipschitz constants. For all sufficiently large $j$, their supports lie compactly inside $B(0,A_j)$, so <a href="#eq:flat-limit-source-oscillation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:flat-limit-source-oscillation">[eq:flat-limit-source-oscillation]</a>, applied to $\varphi_j/C$, gives $|\mathcal R_{\mu_j}(\varphi_j)|\le Cv_j\to0$. Thus $\mathcal R_\nu(\varphi)=0$. This argument applies to every such test on the measure subsequence already selected above.

Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:planar-rigidity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:planar-rigidity">16</a> to this plane-supported limit, using the inherited hard-truncation bound $D_0$. It gives $\nu=q(L_\infty)_\#\mathcal L^n$ with $q>0$. Its upper growth and <a href="#eq:flat-limit-lower-growth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:flat-limit-lower-growth">[eq:flat-limit-lower-growth]</a>, evaluated on a ball centered on the plane, give the two bounds for $q$ in <a href="#eq:flat-limit-measure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:flat-limit-measure">[eq:flat-limit-measure]</a>. Since $|u_{j,i}(x)|\le|N_j^*(x-a_j)|=\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(x,S_j)$, the normal moment bounds follow from those of $S_j$.

Finally, convergence of the affine projections is uniform on bounded sets, so <a href="#eq:moving-support-tube" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:moving-support-tube">[eq:moving-support-tube]</a> gives uniform closeness of source support points to the limiting plane. Conversely, if points $y_j$ of that plane in a fixed bounded ball stayed a positive distance from $E_j$, a subsequence would converge to a point $y$ of the plane. A sufficiently small ball about $y$ would then miss $E_j$ for all large $j$. A nonnegative compact test in that ball with positive integral against $q(L_\infty)_\#\mathcal L^n$ contradicts weak convergence. This proves the stated bilateral convergence. ◻

</details>

In the later contradiction argument we take $\gamma=1/4$, $\delta_j=2^{-j}$, $K_j=2j+3$, $A_j=8\cdot2^{K_j}$, and $v_j=\delta_j^3$. These choices satisfy $\delta_j2^{\gamma K_j}\to0$ as well as $\delta_j^{-1}2^{-K_j}\to0$. The latter condition will control the normalized exterior remainder; it was not needed to identify the unnormalized planar measure.

<a id="sec:height-compactness"></a>
### Fractional energy compactness on moving measures

The planar limit identifies the support at order one. To improve the *normalized* excess, we must retain the first-order normal displacement. Two ingredients do this: a positive energy estimate obtained by testing with an unnormalized height, and a finite-partition compactness argument for functions whose underlying measures vary. We state the resulting convergence in terms of measures and moments; its formulation will also justify the singular limiting equation in the next section.

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
#### A local energy estimate

For a measure $\sigma$, a bounded measurable set $U$, and a real measurable function $f$, write <a id="eq:height-energy-definition"></a>$$\mathcal E_{\sigma,U}(f)
 =\iint_{U\times U}
       \frac{|f(x)-f(y)|^2}{|x-y|^{n+1}}\,d\sigma(x)d\sigma(y),
 \label{eq:height-energy-definition}$$ when this nonnegative integral is finite. Its integrand is assigned value zero on the diagonal. Positive-dimensional upper growth implies that singletons have measure zero, so this convention has no effect.

The following statement uses the geometric and moment hypotheses supplied by Proposition <a href="#prop:flat-limit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:flat-limit">17</a>, together with explicit oscillation and horizon conditions. The small parameter is denoted by $\delta_j$; the integer $K_j$ records the last available dyadic moment.

<a id="stmt-lem-height-energy"></a>
<div id="lem:height-energy" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:height-growth"></a>
<a id="eq:height-affine-data"></a>
<a id="eq:height-last-radius"></a>
<a id="eq:height-small-oscillation"></a>
**Lemma 18** (Energy of a normalized affine height). *Let $1\le n<d$, $0<\gamma<1$, and let $\mu_j$ be Radon measures on $\mathbb R^d$ with $0\in\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\mu_j$. Suppose that, for fixed $G,c>0$, $$\begin{aligned}
 \mu_j(B(x,r))&\le Gr^n
       &&(x\in\mathbb R^d, r>0),\notag\\
 \mu_j(B(0,r))&\ge cr^n
       &&(0<r\le\mathop{\mathrm{diam}}\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\mu_j),
 \label{eq:height-growth}
\end{aligned}$$ and $\mathop{\mathrm{diam}}\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\mu_j\to\infty$. Let $u_j$ be real affine functions with $$u_j(x)-u_j(y)=\langle e_j,x-y\rangle,
 \qquad |e_j|\le1,\qquad |u_j(0)|\le W.
 \label{eq:height-affine-data}$$ Assume $\delta_j>0$, $\delta_j\to0$, $K_j\to\infty$, and $$\frac{2^{-K_j}}{\delta_j}\longrightarrow0.
 \label{eq:height-last-radius}$$ Suppose, with a fixed $M<\infty$, that <a id="eq:height-dyadic-moments"></a>$$\int_{B(0,2^k)}|u_j|^2\,d\mu_j
 \le M\delta_j^2 2^{(n+2+2\gamma)k}
 \qquad(0\le k\le K_j).
 \label{eq:height-dyadic-moments}$$ Finally, let $A_j\to\infty$ and suppose that every compactly supported Lipschitz function $\phi$ with $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\phi\subset B(0,A_j)$ and $\int\phi\,d\mu_j=0$ satisfies $$|\mathcal R_{\mu_j}(\phi)|
       \le \delta_j^3\mathop{\mathrm{Lip}}(\phi).
 \label{eq:height-small-oscillation}$$ Then $w_j=u_j/\delta_j$ has finite local energy, and for every $H<\infty$ there is $C_H<\infty$, independent of $j$, such that <a id="eq:height-uniform-energy"></a>$$\int_{B(0,H)}|w_j|^2\,d\mu_j
       +\mathcal E_{\mu_j,B(0,H)}(w_j)\le C_H.
 \label{eq:height-uniform-energy}$$ The eventual bound depends only on $H,n,\gamma,G,c,M,W$; finitely many initial terms may be included by enlarging $C_H$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-height-energy"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-height-energy</summary>

*Proof.* It suffices to consider $H\ge1$. Fix a Lipschitz function $\chi$ with $0\le\chi\le1$, $\mathop{\mathrm{Lip}}(\chi)\le2/H$, equal to one on $B(0,H)$, and zero outside $B(0,3H/2)$. Its support lies in $B(0,2H)$. Choose the smallest dyadic radius $R\ge4H$, and put $D=B(0,R)$. All estimates below concern sufficiently large $j$, so that $K_j\ge\log_2R$, $\delta_j\le1$, $A_j>2H$, and the lower estimate in <a href="#eq:height-growth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:height-growth">[eq:height-growth]</a> is available at radius $H$. Constants may depend on the fixed quantities displayed in the lemma and on $H,R$, but not on $j$. We omit the index $j$ temporarily.

<a id="eq:height-cutoff-mass"></a>
<a id="eq:height-centered-bounds"></a>
<a id="eq:height-tested-pairing"></a>
The moment bound at radius $R$ gives $$\int_D u^2\,d\mu\le C\delta^2,
 \qquad
 \int\chi^2\,d\mu\ge\mu(B(0,H))\ge cH^n>0.
 \label{eq:height-cutoff-mass}$$ Define the weighted center and its centered test by <a id="eq:height-centered-test"></a>$$b=\frac{\int\chi^2u\,d\mu}{\int\chi^2\,d\mu},
 \qquad U=u-b,\qquad \phi=\chi^2U.
 \label{eq:height-centered-test}$$ Cauchy–Schwarz, followed by <a href="#eq:height-cutoff-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:height-cutoff-mass">[eq:height-cutoff-mass]</a>, proves $$|b|\le C\delta,\qquad
 \int_DU^2\,d\mu\le C\delta^2,\qquad
 \int|\phi|\,d\mu\le C\delta,
 \qquad \int|\phi U|\,d\mu\le C\delta^2.
 \label{eq:height-centered-bounds}$$ In particular, these estimates precede any energy bound. The function $\phi$ has mean zero. It also has a Lipschitz constant bounded independently of $j$: on the support of $\chi$, $|U(x)|\le W+2H+C$, and the product rule for Lipschitz functions gives the bound. The same bound across the complement follows because $\chi$ vanishes there. Consequently <a href="#eq:height-small-oscillation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:height-small-oscillation">[eq:height-small-oscillation]</a> implies $$|\langle e,\mathcal R_\mu(\phi)\rangle|\le C\delta^3.
 \label{eq:height-tested-pairing}$$

<a id="eq:height-cutoff-identity"></a>
<a id="eq:height-near-row"></a>
<a id="eq:height-cutoff-error"></a>
The positive part of this pairing comes from the exact identity $$\begin{aligned}
 &(U(x)-U(y))(\chi(x)^2U(x)-\chi(y)^2U(y))\notag\\
 &\hspace{12mm}=
     |\chi(x)U(x)-\chi(y)U(y)|^2
       -U(x)U(y)(\chi(x)-\chi(y))^2.
 \label{eq:height-cutoff-identity}
\end{aligned}$$ All terms in its integral over $D\times D$ are integrable before a quantitative energy estimate is made. Indeed, a Lipschitz difference cancels one power of the distance in each factor. For every $x\in D$, upper growth and dyadic annuli imply $$\int_D |x-y|^{1-n}\,d\mu(y)\le C G R.
 \label{eq:height-near-row}$$ The integrability of local affine and cutoff energies follows from this bound and the finiteness of $\mu(D)$. For the signed cutoff error, symmetry and $2|U(x)U(y)|\le U(x)^2+U(y)^2$ give the sharper estimate $$\begin{aligned}
 &\iint_{D\times D}
   |U(x)U(y)|\frac{|\chi(x)-\chi(y)|^2}{|x-y|^{n+1}}
                      \,d\mu(x)d\mu(y)\notag\\
 &\hspace{12mm}\le C G R\mathop{\mathrm{Lip}}(\chi)^2\int_D U^2\,d\mu
       \le C\delta^2.
 \label{eq:height-cutoff-error}
\end{aligned}$$ This is an estimate from the already known second moment; it assumes no bound on the unknown fractional energy.

<a id="eq:height-pairing-split"></a>
<a id="eq:height-exterior-cancelled"></a>
We next separate the local pairing from its exterior remainder. Set $q(x,y)=|x-y|^{-n-1}$. The affine identity in <a href="#eq:height-affine-data" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:height-affine-data">[eq:height-affine-data]</a>, and the pairing formula in Lemma <a href="#lem:pairings" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:pairings">5</a>, give $$\begin{aligned}
 \langle e,\mathcal R_\mu(\phi)\rangle
   &=\frac12\iint_{D\times D}
        (U(x)-U(y))(\phi(x)-\phi(y))q(x,y)
                         \,d\mu(x)d\mu(y)+\mathrm{Ext},
 \label{eq:height-pairing-split}\\
 \mathrm{Ext}
   &=\int_D\phi(x)\int_{D^c}
       \bigl[U(x)q(x,y)
           -U(y)\{q(x,y)-q(0,y)\}\bigr]\,d\mu(y)d\mu(x).
 \label{eq:height-exterior-cancelled}
\end{aligned}$$ To obtain the second identity, the base-point subtraction initially also contributes $-U(0)q(0,y)$. That term is separately integrable on $D^c$ and disappears because $\int\phi\,d\mu=0$. The difference multiplying $U(y)$ in <a href="#eq:height-exterior-cancelled" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:height-exterior-cancelled">[eq:height-exterior-cancelled]</a> is kept together.

<a id="eq:height-tail-kernel"></a>
<a id="eq:height-energy-reduction"></a>
<a id="eq:height-uncontrolled-tail"></a>
<a id="eq:height-exterior-bound"></a>
For $|x|\le2H$ and $|y|\ge R$, $$q(x,y)\le C|y|^{-n-1},
 \qquad |q(x,y)-q(0,y)|\le CH|y|^{-n-2}.
 \label{eq:height-tail-kernel}$$ The global affine bound $|U(y)|\le |y|+W+C$, together with upper growth and <a href="#eq:height-tail-kernel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:height-tail-kernel">[eq:height-tail-kernel]</a>, makes the exterior expression absolutely integrable. We may therefore integrate <a href="#eq:height-cutoff-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:height-cutoff-identity">[eq:height-cutoff-identity]</a> and use <a href="#eq:height-pairing-split" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:height-pairing-split">[eq:height-pairing-split]</a> and <a href="#eq:height-cutoff-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:height-cutoff-error">[eq:height-cutoff-error]</a> to obtain $$\mathcal E_{\mu,D}(\chi U)
 \le 2|\langle e,\mathcal R_\mu(\phi)\rangle|
       +2|\mathrm{Ext}|+C\delta^2.
 \label{eq:height-energy-reduction}$$ It remains to bound the exterior remainder at the scale of the known second moment. Put $T=2^K$. Upper growth and dyadic annuli give $\int_{D^c}|y|^{-n-1}\,d\mu(y)\le C/R$. On a controlled shell $t\le |y|<2t$, where $t=2^k$ and $k<K$, Cauchy–Schwarz and the moment at radius $2t$ give <a id="eq:height-shell-first-moment"></a>$$\int_{t\le|y|<2t}|U(y)|\,d\mu(y)
          \le C\delta t^{n+1+\gamma}
 \qquad(t\ge R).
 \label{eq:height-shell-first-moment}$$ Here the contribution of $b$ is absorbed using $t\ge1$. Since $\gamma<1$, summing the controlled shells gives <a id="eq:height-controlled-tail"></a>$$\int_{R\le|y|<T}\frac{|U(y)|}{|y|^{n+2}}\,d\mu(y)
       \le C\delta\sum_{\substack{t\ge R\\t\text{ dyadic}}}t^{\gamma-1}
       \le C\delta.
 \label{eq:height-controlled-tail}$$ Beyond the actual last controlled radius, use the global affine bound $|U(y)|\le |y|+W+C$. Upper growth then gives $$\int_{|y|\ge T}\frac{|U(y)|}{|y|^{n+2}}\,d\mu(y)
       \le C(T^{-1}+T^{-2})\le C T^{-1}.
 \label{eq:height-uncontrolled-tail}$$ The half-open shells and the closed final exterior cover every point, including their boundary spheres. Combining <a href="#eq:height-centered-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:height-centered-bounds">[eq:height-centered-bounds]</a> with the kernel and tail bounds <a href="#eq:height-tail-kernel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:height-tail-kernel">[eq:height-tail-kernel]</a>–<a href="#eq:height-uncontrolled-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:height-uncontrolled-tail">[eq:height-uncontrolled-tail]</a> gives the quantitative estimate $$|\mathrm{Ext}|\le C(\delta^2+\delta T^{-1}).
 \label{eq:height-exterior-bound}$$ Thus the remote part retains the factor $\delta$ supplied by the centered test. Replacing that factor by a constant would lose the required normalization.

The reduction <a href="#eq:height-energy-reduction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:height-energy-reduction">[eq:height-energy-reduction]</a>, the tested-pairing bound <a href="#eq:height-tested-pairing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:height-tested-pairing">[eq:height-tested-pairing]</a>, and <a href="#eq:height-exterior-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:height-exterior-bound">[eq:height-exterior-bound]</a> now yield <a id="eq:height-unnormalized-energy"></a>$$\mathcal E_{\mu,D}(\chi U)
       \le C(\delta^3+\delta^2+\delta T^{-1}).
 \label{eq:height-unnormalized-energy}$$ On $B(0,H)$, $\chi=1$ and the centered difference $U(x)-U(y)$ is $u(x)-u(y)$. Restriction to this smaller product set and division by $\delta^2>0$ give <a id="eq:height-normalized-bound"></a>$$\mathcal E_{\mu,B(0,H)}(u/\delta)
       \le C\left(1+\delta+\frac{1}{\delta T}\right).
 \label{eq:height-normalized-bound}$$ The last term is bounded by <a href="#eq:height-last-radius" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:height-last-radius">[eq:height-last-radius]</a>. The local $L^2$ estimate follows directly from the moment bound at a fixed dyadic radius containing $H$.

For each of the finitely many omitted indices, $u_j/\delta_j$ is an ordinary Lipschitz function. Upper growth gives its finite $L^2$ norm on every fixed ball, and <a href="#eq:height-near-row" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:height-near-row">[eq:height-near-row]</a> gives its finite local energy. Enlarging the bound to cover those indices completes the proof. ◻

</details>

In our application $\delta_j=2^{-j}$ and $K_j=2j+3$. In particular, $\delta_j^{-1}2^{-K_j}=2^{-j-3}\to0$. The conclusion of Lemma <a href="#lem:height-energy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:height-energy">18</a> is a *bounded* normalized energy; no vanishing of that energy has been asserted.

<a id="pm-h-24"></a>
#### Finite partitions on bounded projection regions

<a id="eq:height-projection-regions"></a>
<a id="eq:height-region-null-boundary"></a>
Fix a linear $n$-plane $L$ through the origin, an isometry $e:\mathbb R^n\to L$, and the orthogonal coordinate map $\pi=e^*$, so that $e\pi$ is the orthogonal projection onto $L$. Set $$Q_H=(-(H+1),H+1)^n,
 \qquad
 \Omega_H=\pi^{-1}(Q_H)\cap
 B\bigl(0,(\sqrt n+1)(H+1)\bigr),
 \qquad H=0,1,2,\ldots .
 \label{eq:height-projection-regions}$$ These bounded open sets exhaust $\mathbb R^d$. Their intersection with $L$ is $e(Q_H)$: the closed box $e(\overline Q_H)$ lies strictly inside the ambient bounding ball, because $|e(z)|\le\sqrt n(H+1)$ there. Consequently $$\nu(\partial\Omega_H)=0
 \quad\text{for}\quad \nu=q\,e_\#\mathcal L^n,\quad q>0.
 \label{eq:height-region-null-boundary}$$ Only the coordinate faces meet the plane on this boundary, and those faces have zero $n$-dimensional volume.

<a id="stmt-lem-moving-compactness"></a>
<div id="lem:moving-compactness" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:height-flat-measure-limit"></a>
<a id="eq:height-compactness-hypothesis"></a>
<a id="eq:height-signed-moment-limit"></a>
<a id="eq:height-square-moment-limit"></a>
<a id="eq:height-norm-limit"></a>
**Lemma 19** (Compactness with moving measures). *Suppose $\mu_j$ are Radon measures with a common global upper $n$-growth bound and a common lower bound $cr^n$ at every support point and every admissible radius. Suppose also that $\mathop{\mathrm{diam}}\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\mu_j\to\infty$ and $$\mu_j\longrightarrow\nu=q\,e_\#\mathcal L^n,
       \qquad q>0,
 \label{eq:height-flat-measure-limit}$$ against continuous compactly supported tests. Let $w_j^1,\ldots,w_j^s$ be finitely many real measurable functions. Assume that each belongs to $L^2$ on bounded balls and has integrable local fractional energy, and that for every $R<\infty$ $$\sup_j\sum_{a=1}^s
 \left(\int_{B(0,R)}|w_j^a|^2\,d\mu_j
        +\mathcal E_{\mu_j,B(0,R)}(w_j^a)\right)<\infty.
 \label{eq:height-compactness-hypothesis}$$ There are one subsequence and measurable functions $v^1,\ldots,v^s\in L^2_{\mathrm{loc}}(L,\nu)$ such that, on that subsequence, for every $H$ and every continuous function $\psi$ on the compact set $\overline\Omega_H$, $$\begin{aligned}
 \int_{\Omega_H}w_j^a\psi\,d\mu_j
       &\longrightarrow\int_{\Omega_H}v^a\psi\,d\nu,
 \label{eq:height-signed-moment-limit}\\
 \int_{\Omega_H}|w_j^a|^2\psi\,d\mu_j
       &\longrightarrow\int_{\Omega_H}|v^a|^2\psi\,d\nu.
 \label{eq:height-square-moment-limit}
\end{aligned}$$ The ordinary restrictions $\mu_j|_{\Omega_H}$ also converge weakly to $\nu|_{\Omega_H}$ as finite measures on $\overline\Omega_H$. In particular, $$\int_{\Omega_H}|w_j^a|^2\,d\mu_j
       \longrightarrow\int_{\Omega_H}|v^a|^2\,d\nu.
 \label{eq:height-norm-limit}$$ The subsequence and the functions are common to all these localizations and tests.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-moving-compactness"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-moving-compactness</summary>

*Proof.* We give the construction, including the norm convergence. On each bounded projection region, fractional energy controls the variance inside small cells. A common subsequence of their finitely many averages will recover both first and second moments, not just weak convergence of the heights. First, <a href="#eq:height-region-null-boundary" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:height-region-null-boundary">[eq:height-region-null-boundary]</a> and compact support cutoffs imply finite-measure weak convergence on each $\Omega_H$. Indeed, multiply by a compact continuous cutoff equal to one on $\overline\Omega_H$; weak convergence of these finite measures passes to restriction to the continuity set $\Omega_H$. In particular their masses are bounded.

<a id="eq:height-physical-tube"></a>
We will also use the consequence $$\sup\{\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(x,L):x\in\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\mu_j\cap B(0,R)\}
                       \longrightarrow0
 \quad(R<\infty).
 \label{eq:height-physical-tube}$$ If this failed, some bounded sequence of support points would stay a positive distance from $L$. Passing to a subsequence, a fixed small ball about its limit would be disjoint from $L$ and would contain a smaller ball about each of those support points. The lower growth bound would give the smaller ball a fixed positive mass; its radius is admissible for large $j$. A continuous compact cutoff, equal to one on all the smaller balls and supported away from $L$, would then contradict <a href="#eq:height-flat-measure-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:height-flat-measure-limit">[eq:height-flat-measure-limit]</a>. The cutoff is taken on a larger ambient ball, so the smaller support balls retain their full mass even if they cross a localization boundary.

<a id="eq:height-cell-mass-limits"></a>
Fix $H$, and partition the half-open box $(-(H+1),H+1]^n$ into equal dyadic boxes of side length $h=2(H+1)2^{-k}$. For such a box $D$, set $$C_D=\Omega_H\cap\pi^{-1}(D).$$ These finitely many disjoint measurable cells cover $\Omega_H$. Each cell boundary has $\nu$-measure zero, by <a href="#eq:height-region-null-boundary" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:height-region-null-boundary">[eq:height-region-null-boundary]</a> and the nullity of coordinate faces. The same holds for intersections of cells from different partition levels. Hence $$\mu_j(C_D)\longrightarrow\nu(C_D)=q h^n,
 \qquad
 \mu_j(C_D\cap C_{D'})\longrightarrow\nu(C_D\cap C_{D'}).
 \label{eq:height-cell-mass-limits}$$ For each fixed partition level, all its cell masses are therefore at least $(q/2)h^n$ for sufficiently large $j$. This uses positivity on *every* full-plane cell, rather than just nonzero total mass. By <a href="#eq:height-physical-tube" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:height-physical-tube">[eq:height-physical-tube]</a>, at that same fixed level the actual support points in one cell have mutual distance at most $(\sqrt n+2)h$ for sufficiently large $j$. The entire projection cylinder need not have bounded diameter; only pairs seen by the restricted measure are used.

<a id="eq:height-cell-variance"></a>
<a id="eq:height-partition-error"></a>
For $w_j=w_j^a$, let $m_{j,D}$ be its average on $C_D$ (zero if the cell has zero mass), and let $P_k^jw_j$ be the corresponding step function. The exact variance identity gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \int_{C_D}|w_j-m_{j,D}|^2\,d\mu_j
 &=\frac{1}{2\mu_j(C_D)}
    \iint_{C_D\times C_D}|w_j(x)-w_j(y)|^2\,d\mu_j(x)d\mu_j(y)
       \notag\\
 &\le C_n q^{-1}h
    \iint_{C_D\times C_D}
       \frac{|w_j(x)-w_j(y)|^2}{|x-y|^{n+1}}
                     \,d\mu_j(x)d\mu_j(y).
 \label{eq:height-cell-variance}
\end{aligned}$$ All averages are defined for these sufficiently large indices, and their $L^2$ bounds follow from <a href="#eq:height-compactness-hypothesis" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:height-compactness-hypothesis">[eq:height-compactness-hypothesis]</a>. The sets $C_D\times C_D$ are disjoint subsets of $\Omega_H\times\Omega_H$. Summing <a href="#eq:height-cell-variance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:height-cell-variance">[eq:height-cell-variance]</a> yields $$\int_{\Omega_H}|w_j-P_k^jw_j|^2\,d\mu_j
                 \le C_H h.
 \label{eq:height-partition-error}$$ There is no factor counting the cells in this estimate.

For fixed $H,k,D,a$, Cauchy–Schwarz and the positive limiting cell mass bound $m_{j,D}^a$. A diagonal subsequence therefore makes all these averages converge simultaneously: the collection of indices is countable, including all $H,k$ and the finite set of coordinates $a$. Write $m_D^a$ for their limits and $P_{H,k}^a$ for the corresponding step function on the limiting plane box.

At fixed levels $k,l$, expand the squared difference of the two step functions. Convergence of the finitely many coefficients, cell masses, and intersection masses in <a href="#eq:height-cell-mass-limits" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:height-cell-mass-limits">[eq:height-cell-mass-limits]</a> gives $$\int_{\Omega_H}|P_k^jw_j^a-P_l^jw_j^a|^2\,d\mu_j
       \longrightarrow
       \int_{\Omega_H}|P_{H,k}^a-P_{H,l}^a|^2\,d\nu.$$ The triangle inequality and <a href="#eq:height-partition-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:height-partition-error">[eq:height-partition-error]</a> show that the last integral is at most $2C_H(h_k+h_l)$. The step functions thus converge in $L^2(\nu|_{\Omega_H})$ to some $v_H^a$.

The reverse triangle inequality also gives $$\left|\|w_j^a\|_{L^2(\mu_j|_{\Omega_H})}
       -\|P_k^jw_j^a\|_{L^2(\mu_j|_{\Omega_H})}\right|
       \le (C_H h_k)^{1/2}.$$ For fixed $k$, the step-function norm converges, since its square is the finite sum $\sum_D(m_{j,D}^a)^2\mu_j(C_D)$. First let $j\to\infty$, then $k\to\infty$. This proves <a href="#eq:height-norm-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:height-norm-limit">[eq:height-norm-limit]</a> with $v_H^a$ in place of $v^a$.

For a continuous $\psi$ on $\overline\Omega_H$, the signed testing error from replacing $w_j^a$ by $P_k^jw_j^a$ is at most $$(C_Hh_k)^{1/2}\|\psi\|_\infty\mu_j(\Omega_H)^{1/2}.$$ For fixed $k$, weak convergence on the continuity cells gives $\int_{C_D}\psi\,d\mu_j\to\int_{C_D}\psi\,d\nu$. Passing first in $j$, then in $k$, proves <a href="#eq:height-signed-moment-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:height-signed-moment-limit">[eq:height-signed-moment-limit]</a> for $v_H^a$.

The local limits agree on overlaps. To see this without choosing new subsequences, let $U=\Omega_H\cap\Omega_J$ and $\eta(x)=\min\{1,\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(x,U^c)\}$. This bounded Lipschitz function is positive exactly on $U$. For a compactly supported Lipschitz $\psi$, use $\eta\psi$ in the two signed-moment limits. The source integrals are identical, since $\eta\psi$ vanishes outside the overlap. Their limits imply $$\int_U\eta(v_H^a-v_J^a)\psi\,d\nu=0.$$ Compact Lipschitz tests are dense in $L^2(\nu|_U)$, so $\eta(v_H^a-v_J^a)=0$ almost everywhere. Since $\eta>0$ on $U$, the local limits agree there. Choosing measurable representatives and, on the plane, using the first region containing a point now produces one measurable $v^a$ agreeing with every $v_H^a$ almost everywhere. Countability of the cover ensures this simultaneous agreement.

It remains to prove the weighted squared-moment conclusion. Fix $H,a$, and abbreviate $w_j=w_j^a$, $v=v^a$. There are bounded compactly supported ambient Lipschitz functions $g$ whose restrictions approximate $v$ in $L^2(\nu|_{\Omega_H})$. For completeness, simple functions are dense in this space; regularity of finite plane volume approximates their sets by compact and open sets, and distance cutoffs approximate their indicators. Extend the resulting plane Lipschitz functions by $\pi$, and multiply by a fixed ambient compact cutoff equal to one on $\overline\Omega_H$.

<a id="eq:height-continuous-approximation"></a>
For each such fixed $g$, the norm limit, the signed limit tested against $g$, and ordinary weak convergence tested against $g^2$ give $$\lim_j\int_{\Omega_H}|w_j-g|^2\,d\mu_j
       =\int_{\Omega_H}|v-g|^2\,d\nu.
 \label{eq:height-continuous-approximation}$$ This identity involves $g$, an actual continuous function, on the moving supports. For any continuous weight $\psi$, $$\begin{aligned}
 \left|\int_{\Omega_H}\psi(w_j^2-g^2)\,d\mu_j\right|
 &\le \|\psi\|_\infty
     \|w_j-g\|_{L^2(\mu_j|_{\Omega_H})}\\
 &\hspace{8mm}\cdot
     \left(\|w_j\|_{L^2(\mu_j|_{\Omega_H})}
                  +\|g\|_{L^2(\mu_j|_{\Omega_H})}\right).
\end{aligned}$$ The last factor remains bounded for an $L^2$-approximating family $g$, after taking $j\to\infty$ for each fixed $g$. Ordinary weak convergence handles $\psi g^2$. Equation <a href="#eq:height-continuous-approximation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:height-continuous-approximation">[eq:height-continuous-approximation]</a> and Cauchy–Schwarz for the limiting measure then allow $g\to v$ in $L^2(\nu|_{\Omega_H})$. This proves <a href="#eq:height-square-moment-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:height-square-moment-limit">[eq:height-square-moment-limit]</a> in the stated order of limits. ◻

</details>

The lemma may equivalently be read as three finite-measure convergences on each common compact closure: <a id="eq:height-three-measures"></a>$$\mu_j|_{\Omega_H}\ \longrightarrow\ \nu|_{\Omega_H},\qquad
 w_j^a\mu_j|_{\Omega_H}\ \longrightarrow\ v^a\nu|_{\Omega_H},\qquad
 |w_j^a|^2\mu_j|_{\Omega_H}\ \longrightarrow\ |v^a|^2\nu|_{\Omega_H}.
 \label{eq:height-three-measures}$$ The middle measures are signed; their total variations are uniformly bounded by Cauchy–Schwarz. These statements do not require evaluating an arbitrary representative of $v^a$ against $\mu_j$. Once the next section identifies $v^a$ with an affine function, the same polarization as in <a href="#eq:height-continuous-approximation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:height-continuous-approximation">[eq:height-continuous-approximation]</a> will give a genuine squared-error limit for that fixed affine representative.

<a id="sec:fractional-rigidity"></a>
### The renormalized height equation and affine rigidity

The compactness lemma produces a single limiting height. We now pass the small Riesz pairing to that height, keeping the cancellation at infinity throughout the passage. The resulting equation is tested only against functions of integral zero. That class is sufficient to locate the Fourier transform at the origin and hence to prove that the height is affine. Interpreting a fractional operator modulo constants is natural for functions of polynomial growth; compare (Dipierro, Savin, and Valdinoci 2019) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[DipierroSavinValdinoci2019](#ref-DipierroSavinValdinoci2019)]. We prove the measurable weak formulation needed here directly, rather than importing a regularity or equivalence statement from a different formulation.

<a id="pm-h-26"></a>
#### A fixed normalization for the height pairing

<a id="eq:height-weighted-tail"></a>
<a id="eq:normalized-height-pairing"></a>
Write $k(z)=|z|^{-n-1}$ for $z\ne0$. Let $\rho$ be a measure with upper $n$-growth, and let $w$ be a measurable function with finite local $L^2(\rho)$ norm and finite local energy $$\iint_{B(0,T)^2}|w(x)-w(y)|^2 k(x-y)\,d\rho(x)d\rho(y)<\infty
 \qquad(T>0).$$ Suppose also that $$\label{eq:height-weighted-tail}
 \int_{|y|>T}|w(y)|\,|y|^{-n-2}\,d\rho(y)<\infty
 \qquad(T>0).$$ For a compactly supported Lipschitz function $\phi$, choose a bounded measurable set $A$ containing a ball $B(0,R)$, with $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\phi\subset B(0,H)$ and $2H<R$. Define $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathcal B_{\rho,A}(w,\phi)
 ={}&\frac12\iint_{A\times A}
       (w(x)-w(y))(\phi(x)-\phi(y))k(x-y)\,d\rho(x)d\rho(y)
       \label{eq:normalized-height-pairing}\\
 &+\iint_{A\times A^c}\phi(x)
       \bigl[w(x)k(x-y)-w(y)\{k(x-y)-k(-y)\}\bigr]
       \,d\rho(x)d\rho(y).\notag
\end{aligned}$$ This expression is defined even when $\int\phi\,d\rho\ne0$. The set $A$ and the subtraction point $0$ are part of its normalization.

<a id="eq:height-diagonal-estimate"></a>
<a id="eq:height-kernel-tail"></a>
Both integrals in <a href="#eq:normalized-height-pairing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:normalized-height-pairing">[eq:normalized-height-pairing]</a> are absolutely convergent. For the interior integral, Cauchy–Schwarz, the Lipschitz bound, and upper growth give, for every $h>0$, $$\begin{aligned}
 &\iint_{\substack{x,y\in A\\|x-y|\le h}}
   |w(x)-w(y)|\,|\phi(x)-\phi(y)|k(x-y)\,d\rho(x)d\rho(y)
   \label{eq:height-diagonal-estimate}\\
 &\hspace{15mm}\le
  C\,\mathop{\mathrm{Lip}}(\phi)
  \left(\iint_{A^2}|w(x)-w(y)|^2k(x-y)\,d\rho(x)d\rho(y)\right)^{1/2}
  \rho(A)^{1/2}h^{1/2}.\notag
\end{aligned}$$ Indeed, the square of the remaining factor is bounded by $$\mathop{\mathrm{Lip}}(\phi)^2\iint_{A^2\cap\{|x-y|\le h\}}|x-y|^{1-n}
 \,d\rho(x)d\rho(y)\le C\mathop{\mathrm{Lip}}(\phi)^2\rho(A)h.$$ The part separated from the diagonal follows from local $L^1$ integrability. For $x\in\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\phi$ and $|y|\ge R$, the mean value theorem gives $$\label{eq:height-kernel-tail}
 k(x-y)\le C|y|^{-n-1},\qquad
 |k(x-y)-k(-y)|\le C H|y|^{-n-2}.$$ The first term is integrable by upper growth and $\phi w\in L^1(\rho)$; the second uses <a href="#eq:height-weighted-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:height-weighted-tail">[eq:height-weighted-tail]</a>.

When $\int\phi\,d\rho=0$, the value in <a href="#eq:normalized-height-pairing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:normalized-height-pairing">[eq:normalized-height-pairing]</a> is independent of the sufficiently large localization $A$. To see this without subtracting two divergent integrals, restrict $\rho$ first to a common finite outer ball. Symmetry splits the half double integral into its $A^2$ part and the cross term. The added center term integrates to $$\left(\int\phi\,d\rho\right)
 \left(\int_{A^c}w(y)k(-y)\,d\rho(y)\right)=0$$ in that finite restriction. For two choices of $A$, both expressions therefore equal the same finite double integral. Now increase the outer ball; <a href="#eq:height-kernel-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:height-kernel-tail">[eq:height-kernel-tail]</a> and <a href="#eq:height-weighted-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:height-weighted-tail">[eq:height-weighted-tail]</a> permit dominated convergence of each renormalized exterior term. This proves the asserted independence.

<a id="eq:affine-source-height"></a>
<a id="eq:height-riesz-identification"></a>
There is also an exact connection with the vector pairing of Lemma <a href="#lem:pairings" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:pairings">5</a>. Suppose $$\label{eq:affine-source-height}
 u(x)-u(y)=e\cdot(x-y),\qquad w=u/\delta,\qquad \delta>0.$$ Then for a compact Lipschitz test of $\rho$-mean zero, $$\label{eq:height-riesz-identification}
 \mathcal B_{\rho,A}(w,\phi)
       =\delta^{-1} e\cdot\mathcal R_\rho(\phi).$$ The interior identity follows directly from <a href="#eq:affine-source-height" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:affine-source-height">[eq:affine-source-height]</a>. In the exterior, subtraction of $e\cdot K_n(-y)$ produces $$\delta^{-1}\bigl[u(x)k(x-y)
       -u(y)\{k(x-y)-k(-y)\}-u(0)k(-y)\bigr].$$ The last term integrates to zero against $\phi$. Its exterior kernel is integrable by upper growth and separation from $0$, so this cancellation is legitimate. The identity imposes no bound on $w(0)$.

<a id="pm-h-27"></a>
#### Passing to the same limiting height

<a id="stmt-prop-height-equation"></a>
<div id="prop:height-equation" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:height-source-moment"></a>
<a id="eq:height-source-oscillation"></a>
**Proposition 20** (The limiting height equation). *Assume the hypotheses and conclusions of Lemmas <a href="#lem:height-energy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:height-energy">18</a> and <a href="#lem:moving-compactness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:moving-compactness">19</a> for a scalar height. We retain their notation and record the conditions used below. Let $\mu_j$ converge on compact tests to $\nu=q\mathcal H^n|_L$, where $q>0$ and $L$ is an $n$-plane through $0$. Here $\mathcal H^n|_L$ has Euclidean normalization, equal to Lebesgue measure in orthonormal coordinates on $L$. Let $u_j$ satisfy <a href="#eq:affine-source-height" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:affine-source-height">[eq:affine-source-height]</a> with $|e_j|\le1$, let $w_j=u_j/\delta_j$, and let $v$ be the common limit given by Lemma <a href="#lem:moving-compactness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:moving-compactness">19</a>. Suppose $\delta_j>0$, $\delta_j\to0$, $K_j\to\infty$, and, for some fixed $0<\gamma<1$ and $M<\infty$, $$\label{eq:height-source-moment}
 \int_{B(0,2^k)}|u_j|^2\,d\mu_j
       \le M\delta_j^2\,2^{k(n+2+2\gamma)}
       \quad(0\le k\le K_j),\qquad
 \delta_j^{-1}2^{-K_j}\longrightarrow0.$$ Assume that there are radii $A_j\to\infty$ such that, for every $j$ and every compact Lipschitz test $\xi$ supported in $B(0,A_j)$ with $\int\xi\,d\mu_j=0$, $$\label{eq:height-source-oscillation}
 |\mathcal R_{\mu_j}(\xi)|
      \le C(\mathop{\mathrm{Lip}}\xi+1)\delta_j^3.$$ The constant here is independent of $j$ and of the test.*

<a id="eq:limit-height-weight"></a>
<a id="eq:limit-fractional-equation"></a>
*Then $v$ has finite local fractional energy and satisfies $$\label{eq:limit-height-weight}
 \int_L |v(x)|(1+|x|)^{-n-2}\,d\mathcal H^n(x)<\infty.$$ After identifying $L$ isometrically with $\mathbb R^n$, for every compactly supported smooth complex function $g$ with $\int_{\mathbb R^n}g=0$, $$\label{eq:limit-fractional-equation}
 \int_{\mathbb R^n}v(x)\mathcal Lg(x)\,dx=0,
 \qquad
 \mathcal Lg(x)=\frac12\int_{\mathbb R^n}
       \frac{2g(x)-g(x+h)-g(x-h)}{|h|^{n+1}}\,dh.$$ The integral on the left is absolutely convergent. The same conclusions hold simultaneously for the finite family of normal heights in Lemma <a href="#lem:moving-compactness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:moving-compactness">19</a>, without further extraction.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-height-equation"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-height-equation</summary>

*Proof.* We retain the subsequence already selected in Lemma <a href="#lem:moving-compactness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:moving-compactness">19</a>. In the proof below every bounded region and every test is chosen after this subsequence and its height $v$.

*Local energy and singular pairings.* Let $\Omega$ be one of the bounded continuity regions used in that lemma. On its common compact closure, the three measures $$\mu_j|_\Omega,\qquad w_j\mu_j|_\Omega,
       \qquad w_j^2\mu_j|_\Omega$$ converge against continuous functions to the corresponding measures $\nu|_\Omega$, $v\nu|_\Omega$, and $v^2\nu|_\Omega$. Their total variations are uniformly bounded. Finite sums of products of continuous functions approximate a continuous function on the product of this compact set uniformly. Thus, after replacing $k(x-y)$ by $(\max(h,|x-y|))^{-n-1}$, both the quadratic energy and every bilinear pairing against a fixed bounded Lipschitz test converge. For the energy, expand $(w_j(x)-w_j(y))^2$; its three terms use exactly the three measures above. Monotone convergence as $h\downarrow0$ shows that the limiting energy is no larger than the common bound from Lemma <a href="#lem:height-energy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:height-energy">18</a>. Positive-dimensional upper growth makes the diagonal null.

<a id="eq:height-local-bilinear-limit"></a>
Estimate <a href="#eq:height-diagonal-estimate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:height-diagonal-estimate">[eq:height-diagonal-estimate]</a> applies uniformly to the source and limiting heights on $\Omega$. The error caused by capping the bilinear kernel is bounded by its absolute integral on $|x-y|\le h$. Consequently $$\begin{aligned}
\label{eq:height-local-bilinear-limit}
 &\iint_{\Omega^2}(w_j(x)-w_j(y))(\phi(x)-\phi(y))k(x-y)
       \,d\mu_j(x)d\mu_j(y)\\
 &\qquad\longrightarrow
 \iint_{\Omega^2}(v(x)-v(y))(\phi(x)-\phi(y))k(x-y)
       \,d\nu(x)d\nu(y).\notag
\end{aligned}$$ This argument first fixes $h$, then takes $j\to\infty$, and finally takes $h\downarrow0$.

<a id="eq:limiting-height-tail-bound"></a>
*Inherited moments and the two exterior errors.* The weak convergence of $w_j^2\mu_j$ on an enclosing continuity region, tested with nonnegative cutoffs, passes <a href="#eq:height-source-moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:height-source-moment">[eq:height-source-moment]</a> to the limit at every fixed scale. Changing the radius by at most a factor two gives <a id="eq:limiting-height-moments"></a>$$\label{eq:limiting-height-moments}
 \int_{L\cap B(0,t)}|v|^2\,d\nu\le C t^{n+2+2\gamma}
 \qquad(t\ge1).$$ Each fixed radius eventually lies below $2^{K_j}$; no estimate past that horizon for a fixed sample has been used. Cauchy–Schwarz on dyadic shells, followed by summation of $2^{k(\gamma-1)}$, now gives $$\label{eq:limiting-height-tail-bound}
 \int_{L\cap\{|y|>T\}} |v(y)|\,|y|^{-n-2}\,d\nu(y)
       \le C T^{\gamma-1}\qquad(T\ge2).$$ Together with local $L^2$ integrability and $q>0$, this proves <a href="#eq:limit-height-weight" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:limit-height-weight">[eq:limit-height-weight]</a>.

<a id="eq:two-height-tail-errors"></a>
Fix $H$ containing the supports of the tests to be considered. Their uniform supremum bounds and the local second-moment bound imply uniform bounds on $\int|\phi|\,d\mu_j$ and $\int|\phi w_j|\,d\mu_j$. For $2H<T\le 2^{K_j}$, the same shell calculation using <a href="#eq:height-source-moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:height-source-moment">[eq:height-source-moment]</a> controls the renormalized exterior integral outside $B(0,T)$ by $$\label{eq:two-height-tail-errors}
 C_\phi\bigl(T^{-1}+T^{\gamma-1}
                    +\delta_j^{-1}2^{-K_j}\bigr).$$ Here is the bound beyond the last controlled radius. The affine functions $u_j$ are $1$-Lipschitz. The mass of $B(0,1)$ is bounded below eventually, by convergence to the plane measure, so <a href="#eq:height-source-moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:height-source-moment">[eq:height-source-moment]</a> implies $|u_j(0)|\le1+C\delta_j$. Hence $|u_j(y)|\le C+|y|$. Upper growth gives $$\int_{|y|\ge2^{K_j}}|w_j(y)||y|^{-n-2}\,d\mu_j(y)
      \le C\delta_j^{-1}2^{-K_j}.$$ This bound uses the unnormalized height at $0$; the normalized value $w_j(0)$ may diverge. The $T^{-1}$ term in <a href="#eq:two-height-tail-errors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:two-height-tail-errors">[eq:two-height-tail-errors]</a> comes from the $w_j(x)k(x-y)$ term, whose $x$-integral stays on the fixed test support. The kernel difference in <a href="#eq:height-kernel-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:height-kernel-tail">[eq:height-kernel-tail]</a> gives the remaining terms. Thus the order is to choose $T$ large and then choose $j$ large. The analogous limiting tail tends to zero by <a href="#eq:limiting-height-tail-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:limiting-height-tail-bound">[eq:limiting-height-tail-bound]</a>.

<a id="eq:fixed-normalization-convergence"></a>
*A common normalization and mean correction.* Take two fixed compact ambient Lipschitz functions $\phi,\eta$ such that $$\int\phi\,d\nu=0,\qquad \int\eta\,d\nu\ne0.$$ Choose one continuity region $A$ containing $B(0,R)$ with a positive margin beyond both supports. For each of these tests separately, $$\label{eq:fixed-normalization-convergence}
 \mathcal B_{\mu_j,A}(w_j,\phi)
       \longrightarrow\mathcal B_{\nu,A}(v,\phi),
 \qquad
 \mathcal B_{\mu_j,A}(w_j,\eta)
       \longrightarrow\mathcal B_{\nu,A}(v,\eta).$$ For the interior terms this is <a href="#eq:height-local-bilinear-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:height-local-bilinear-limit">[eq:height-local-bilinear-limit]</a>. For the exterior terms first restrict $y$ to a larger fixed continuity region $B$. The kernels are separated from their singularities on the nonzero test support. To justify the restrictions, let $F_h$ be the closed $h$-neighborhood of $\partial A\cup\partial B$, for small $h>0$, and choose one bounded continuity region containing all these collars. The local second-moment bound, Cauchy–Schwarz, and Portmanteau give $$\limsup_{j\to\infty}\int_{F_h}|w_j|\,d\mu_j
 \le C\bigl(\limsup_{j\to\infty}\mu_j(F_h)\bigr)^{1/2}
 \le C\nu(F_h)^{1/2}\longrightarrow0
 \qquad(h\downarrow0).$$ The final limit uses the $\nu$-nullity of both boundaries; the same conclusion holds for ordinary mass. Thus continuous cutoffs approximate both restrictions, with product errors controlled by these collar masses and the other factor’s bounded total variation. Convergence of $\mu_j$ and $w_j\mu_j$, followed by uniform approximation of the continuous kernels by finite sums of product functions, now gives the exterior limit on $B$. Finally, <a href="#eq:two-height-tail-errors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:two-height-tail-errors">[eq:two-height-tail-errors]</a> and <a href="#eq:limiting-height-tail-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:limiting-height-tail-bound">[eq:limiting-height-tail-bound]</a> remove the outer restriction. Both convergences in <a href="#eq:fixed-normalization-convergence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fixed-normalization-convergence">[eq:fixed-normalization-convergence]</a> concern the normalization <a href="#eq:normalized-height-pairing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:normalized-height-pairing">[eq:normalized-height-pairing]</a>; no Riesz pairing is assigned to a test of nonzero mean.

<a id="eq:compact-renormalized-height-equation"></a>
Set $$c_j=\frac{\int\phi\,d\mu_j}{\int\eta\,d\mu_j},
       \qquad \xi_j=\phi-c_j\eta.$$ The denominator is nonzero eventually, and compact-test convergence gives $c_j\to0$. Eventually $|c_j|\le1$, so $\xi_j$ has a common compact support, a uniform Lipschitz bound, and zero mean against the whole $\mu_j$. By linearity with the same $A$ and the same subtraction point, $$\mathcal B_{\mu_j,A}(w_j,\xi_j)
       =\mathcal B_{\mu_j,A}(w_j,\phi)
        -c_j\mathcal B_{\mu_j,A}(w_j,\eta)
       \longrightarrow\mathcal B_{\nu,A}(v,\phi).$$ The bump pairing on the right is already normalized and converges; the only required fact about its coefficient is $c_j\to0$. On the other hand, <a href="#eq:height-riesz-identification" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:height-riesz-identification">[eq:height-riesz-identification]</a> and <a href="#eq:height-source-oscillation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:height-source-oscillation">[eq:height-source-oscillation]</a> give $|\mathcal B_{\mu_j,A}(w_j,\xi_j)|\le C_{\phi,\eta}\delta_j^2\to0$. We conclude that $$\label{eq:compact-renormalized-height-equation}
 \mathcal B_{\nu,A}(v,\phi)=0
 \qquad\left(\int\phi\,d\nu=0\right).$$

*Intrinsic tests and the fractional operator.* Let $U:\mathbb R^n\to L$ be a linear isometry. For a compactly supported intrinsic Lipschitz function $g$, choose an ambient radial cutoff $\chi$ equal to one on a ball containing $U(\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}g)$, and put $\phi(x)=\chi(x)g(U^*x)$. This is a compact ambient Lipschitz test and $\phi(Ut)=g(t)$ for every $t$. Its plane integral is $q\int g$. A nonnegative compact radial bump positive at $0$ supplies the required $\eta$. Pulling <a href="#eq:compact-renormalized-height-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:compact-renormalized-height-equation">[eq:compact-renormalized-height-equation]</a> back by $U$ and canceling the positive factor $q^2$ therefore gives the same renormalized equation for $v(Ut)$ against every compact intrinsic mean-zero test. Localization independence allows us to take $A=B(0,R)$ in the intrinsic plane. We henceforth write this intrinsic height simply as $v$.

For completeness, we identify this equation with <a href="#eq:limit-fractional-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:limit-fractional-equation">[eq:limit-fractional-equation]</a>. Let $g$ be compactly supported, smooth and of integral zero, first real-valued, with support in $B(0,H)$; choose $R>2H$.
