For $\varepsilon>0$, define $\mathcal L_\varepsilon$ by restricting the $h$-integral in <a href="#eq:limit-fractional-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:limit-fractional-equation">[eq:limit-fractional-equation]</a> to $|h|\ge\varepsilon$. Reflection and translation give the absolutely convergent single-increment formula $$\mathcal L_\varepsilon g(x)
    =\int_{|x-y|\ge\varepsilon}(g(x)-g(y))k(x-y)\,dy.$$ When $\varepsilon<R-H$, symmetrization on $B(0,R)^2$ yields $$\begin{aligned}
 \int v\mathcal L_\varepsilon g
 ={}&\frac12\iint_{B(0,R)^2\cap\{|x-y|\ge\varepsilon\}}
       (v(x)-v(y))(g(x)-g(y))k(x-y)\,dx\,dy\\
 &+\iint_{B(0,R)\times B(0,R)^c}g(x)
       [v(x)k(x-y)-v(y)\{k(x-y)-k(-y)\}]\,dx\,dy.
\end{aligned}$$ To justify the exterior equality, integrate the test variable first: $\int g=0$ permits the subtraction of $k(-y)$, after which <a href="#eq:height-kernel-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:height-kernel-tail">[eq:height-kernel-tail]</a> makes the product integrable. The direct term involving $v(x)$ is separately integrable. This proves the displayed identity without applying Fubini to the uncancelled exterior height term.

The interior limit as $\varepsilon\downarrow0$ follows from <a href="#eq:height-diagonal-estimate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:height-diagonal-estimate">[eq:height-diagonal-estimate]</a>. On bounded $x$, the second difference of $g$ is bounded by $C|h|^2$ near $h=0$, giving an $\varepsilon$-independent bound for $\mathcal L_\varepsilon g$. Outside a fixed ball containing the support, the cutoff is irrelevant and $\int g=0$ gives $|\mathcal L_\varepsilon g(x)|\le C_g(1+|x|)^{-n-2}$ for $0<\varepsilon\le1$. Dominated convergence using <a href="#eq:limit-height-weight" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:limit-height-weight">[eq:limit-height-weight]</a> therefore applies also to the left-hand side. The resulting identity is $\int v\mathcal Lg=\mathcal B_{dx,B(0,R)}(v,g)=0$. Real and imaginary parts give the complex statement. All steps use the same $v$ and the same preselected subsequence, so they apply to every coordinate in a finite normal frame simultaneously. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-28"></a>
#### Affine rigidity under the weighted tail bound

<a id="eq:rigidity-fractional-operator"></a>
We now prove the analytic rigidity statement needed for the limiting heights. Its tail hypothesis allows linear growth. Write $\mathcal S(\mathbb R^n)$ for the complex Schwartz space, with its usual seminorms given by weighted suprema of derivatives. On general Schwartz tests, the same operator is given by $$\label{eq:rigidity-fractional-operator}
 \mathcal Lg(x)
 =-\frac12\int_{\mathbb R^n}
   \frac{g(x+h)+g(x-h)-2g(x)}{|h|^{n+1}}\,dh.$$ The integral is absolutely convergent: the second difference is $O(|h|^2)$ near zero and is bounded for large $h$. The value assigned to the integrand at $h=0$ has no effect.

<a id="stmt-lem-fractional-rigidity"></a>
<div id="lem:fractional-rigidity" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:rigidity-weighted-tail"></a>
<a id="eq:rigidity-compact-equation"></a>
**Lemma 21** (Weighted affine rigidity). *Let $n\ge1$, and let $v:\mathbb R^n\to\mathbb R$ be measurable with $$\label{eq:rigidity-weighted-tail}
 \int_{\mathbb R^n}|v(x)|(1+|x|)^{-n-2}\,dx<\infty.$$ Suppose that, for every $g\in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R^n;\mathbb C)$ with $\int g=0$, $$\label{eq:rigidity-compact-equation}
 \int_{\mathbb R^n}v(x)\mathcal Lg(x)\,dx=0.$$ Then there are $b\in\mathbb R$ and a real linear functional $\ell:\mathbb R^n\to\mathbb R$ such that $v(x)=b+\ell(x)$ almost everywhere. If $v$ is also essentially bounded, then $\ell=0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-fractional-rigidity"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-fractional-rigidity</summary>

<a id="eq:rigidity-schwartz-decay"></a>
*Proof.* We first justify the extension of the test class in <a href="#eq:rigidity-compact-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rigidity-compact-equation">[eq:rigidity-compact-equation]</a>. We will then compute the positive Fourier multiplier away from zero, deduce that the height is a polynomial, and use the weighted tail bound to exclude degrees at least two. For a Schwartz function $g$ put $$p(g)=\sup_{y\in\mathbb R^n}(1+|y|)^{n+3}
       \bigl(|g(y)|+\|D^2g(y)\|\bigr),
 \qquad
 M_1(g)=\int_{\mathbb R^n}|y|\,|g(y)|\,dy.$$ Here $D^2g$ is the second real derivative, with its operator norm. The quantity $p$ is a continuous Schwartz seminorm, and $M_1(g)\le C_n p(g)$, since $\int |y|(1+|y|)^{-n-3}\,dy<\infty$. We claim that $$\label{eq:rigidity-schwartz-decay}
 |\mathcal Lg(x)|\le C_n p(g)(1+|x|)^{-n-2}
 \qquad\text{if }\int g=0.$$ Splitting the increment integral at radius one gives $|\mathcal Lg(x)|\le C_n p(g)$ for every $x$. For the spatial decay take $r=|x|\ge2$ and $R=r/2$. If $|h|<R$, the segments from $x$ to $x\pm h$ stay outside $B(0,r/2)$. Taylor’s formula therefore bounds the near part of <a href="#eq:rigidity-fractional-operator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rigidity-fractional-operator">[eq:rigidity-fractional-operator]</a> in absolute value by $$C_n p(g)r^{-n-3}
      \int_{|h|<R}|h|^{1-n}\,dh
 \le C_n p(g)r^{-n-2}.$$

<a id="eq:rigidity-far-correction"></a>
For the far part let $E_x=\{y:|x-y|\ge R\}$ and $k(z)=|z|^{-n-1}$. Translation and reflection give exactly $$g(x)\int_{|h|\ge R}k(h)\,dh
       -\int_{E_x}k(x-y)g(y)\,dy.$$ These integrals are separately absolutely convergent. The first term is bounded by $C_n|g(x)|/r$. Using the mean-zero condition in the second term gives the identity $$\label{eq:rigidity-far-correction}
 \int_{E_x}k(x-y)g(y)\,dy
 =\int_{E_x}\bigl(k(x-y)-k(x)\bigr)g(y)\,dy
       -k(x)\int_{E_x^c}g(y)\,dy.$$ On $|y|\le r/2$, the mean value theorem gives $$|k(x-y)-k(x)|\le C_n|y|r^{-n-2}.$$ On $E_x\cap\{|y|>r/2\}$, the kernel difference is bounded by $C_n r^{-n-1}$, whereas $$\int_{|y|>r/2}|g(y)|\,dy\le\frac{2}{r}M_1(g).$$ Finally $E_x^c\subset\{|y|>r/2\}$, so the last term in <a href="#eq:rigidity-far-correction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rigidity-far-correction">[eq:rigidity-far-correction]</a> has the same bound $C_n r^{-n-2}M_1(g)$. This includes the correction for the omitted ball around $x$. The near and far estimates prove <a href="#eq:rigidity-schwartz-decay" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rigidity-schwartz-decay">[eq:rigidity-schwartz-decay]</a>.

<a id="eq:rigidity-pairing-continuity"></a>
It follows from <a href="#eq:rigidity-weighted-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rigidity-weighted-tail">[eq:rigidity-weighted-tail]</a> and <a href="#eq:rigidity-schwartz-decay" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rigidity-schwartz-decay">[eq:rigidity-schwartz-decay]</a> that $v\mathcal Lg$ is integrable for every mean-zero Schwartz function, and $$\label{eq:rigidity-pairing-continuity}
 \left|\int v\mathcal Lg\right|
 \le C_n p(g)\int |v(x)|(1+|x|)^{-n-2}\,dx.$$ Choose $\chi,\rho\in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R^n)$ with $\chi=1$ near zero and $\int\rho=1$. For a mean-zero Schwartz function $g$ set $$g_j(x)=\chi(x/j)g(x)
        -\left(\int\chi(y/j)g(y)\,dy\right)\rho(x).$$ Each $g_j$ is compactly supported and has mean zero. The product rule and rapid decrease show $\chi(\cdot/j)g\to g$ in every Schwartz seminorm; also $\int\chi(y/j)g(y)\,dy\to\int g=0$. Thus $g_j\to g$ in Schwartz space. Applying <a href="#eq:rigidity-pairing-continuity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rigidity-pairing-continuity">[eq:rigidity-pairing-continuity]</a> to $g_j-g$ extends <a href="#eq:rigidity-compact-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rigidity-compact-equation">[eq:rigidity-compact-equation]</a> to every mean-zero $g\in\mathcal S(\mathbb R^n)$.

We next compute the Fourier multiplier needed for localization away from zero. Use the convention $$\widehat g(\xi)=\int_{\mathbb R^n}e^{-2\pi i x\cdot\xi}g(x)\,dx,
 \qquad
 \mathcal F^{-1}g(x)=\int_{\mathbb R^n}e^{2\pi i x\cdot\xi}g(\xi)\,d\xi.$$ Define $$a_n(\xi)=\int_{\mathbb R^n}
        \frac{1-\cos(2\pi\xi\cdot h)}{|h|^{n+1}}\,dh.$$ This is an absolutely convergent nonnegative integral: quadratic cancellation controls the origin, and boundedness of $1-\cos$ controls infinity. If $\xi\ne0$, the numerator equals $2$ at $h=\xi/(2|\xi|^2)$ and is positive on a neighborhood of that point. Consequently $a_n(\xi)>0$. Orthogonal changes of variables show that $a_n$ is radial, and the substitution $u=th$ gives $a_n(t\xi)=t a_n(\xi)$ for $t>0$. Hence <a id="eq:rigidity-positive-symbol"></a>$$\label{eq:rigidity-positive-symbol}
 a_n(\xi)=c_n|\xi|,\qquad c_n=a_n(e_1)>0.$$

<a id="eq:rigidity-fourier-multiplier"></a>
For $g\in\mathcal S(\mathbb R^n)$ the full absolute integral required to interchange frequency and increments is finite: $$\int_{\mathbb R^n}\int_{\mathbb R^n}
   \frac{1-\cos(2\pi\xi\cdot h)}{|h|^{n+1}}|g(\xi)|\,dh\,d\xi
 =c_n\int_{\mathbb R^n}|\xi|\,|g(\xi)|\,d\xi<\infty.$$ Fubini’s theorem and the second difference of the exponential therefore give, pointwise in $x$, $$\label{eq:rigidity-fourier-multiplier}
 \mathcal L(\mathcal F^{-1}g)(x)
   =c_n\int_{\mathbb R^n}e^{2\pi i x\cdot\xi}|\xi|g(\xi)\,d\xi.$$ The factor $-2$ in that second difference cancels the factor $-1/2$ in <a href="#eq:rigidity-fractional-operator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rigidity-fractional-operator">[eq:rigidity-fractional-operator]</a>.

The weighted hypothesis implies $v\in L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}$ and defines a tempered distribution $$T_v(g)=\int_{\mathbb R^n}v(x)g(x)\,dx.$$ Indeed this integral is bounded by $\sup_x(1+|x|)^{n+2}|g(x)|$ times the integral in <a href="#eq:rigidity-weighted-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rigidity-weighted-tail">[eq:rigidity-weighted-tail]</a>. All distributional pairings here are complex bilinear, without complex conjugation. Let $S=\mathcal F^{-1}T_v$, so $S(g)=T_v(\mathcal F^{-1}g)$. Take $\psi\in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R^n\setminus\{0\})$. The function $q(\xi)=\psi(\xi)/|\xi|$, extended by zero near the origin, is smooth and compactly supported. Its inverse Fourier transform is Schwartz and has integral $q(0)=0$. The extended weak equation and <a href="#eq:rigidity-fourier-multiplier" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rigidity-fourier-multiplier">[eq:rigidity-fourier-multiplier]</a> give $$0=T_v\bigl(\mathcal L(\mathcal F^{-1}q)\bigr)
   =c_nT_v(\mathcal F^{-1}\psi)=c_n S(\psi).$$ Thus $S$ is supported at the origin.

<a id="eq:rigidity-finite-order"></a>
For completeness we prove the point-support consequence; compare (Hörmander 2003, Theorem 2.3.4) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[Hormander2003](#ref-Hormander2003)]. Continuity of $S$ on Schwartz space supplies $N\in\mathbb N$ and $C<\infty$ such that $$\label{eq:rigidity-finite-order}
 |S(g)|\le C\sum_{|\alpha|\le N}
       \sup_x(1+|x|)^N|\partial^\alpha g(x)|.$$ A Schwartz function vanishing near zero is annihilated by $S$: multiply it by expanding compact cutoffs, use the support property, and pass to the Schwartz limit. Consequently $S$ has the same value on two Schwartz functions agreeing near zero.

Suppose now that $\partial^\alpha g(0)=0$ for every $|\alpha|\le N$. Choose $\eta\in C_c^\infty(B(0,2))$ equal to one near zero and put $\eta_\varepsilon(x)=\eta(x/\varepsilon)$. For $0<\varepsilon<1/2$, Taylor’s formula and the product rule give $$\sup_x(1+|x|)^N
       |\partial^\alpha(\eta_\varepsilon g)(x)|
       \le C_g\varepsilon^{N+1-|\alpha|}
       \le C_g\varepsilon
       \qquad (|\alpha|\le N).$$ To see the power, a derivative of order $j$ falling on the cutoff costs $\varepsilon^{-j}$, while the remaining derivative of $g$, of order $|\alpha|-j$, is $O(\varepsilon^{N+1-|\alpha|+j})$ on its support. Since $S(g)=S(\eta_\varepsilon g)$, letting $\varepsilon\downarrow0$ in <a href="#eq:rigidity-finite-order" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rigidity-finite-order">[eq:rigidity-finite-order]</a> proves $S(g)=0$.

It follows that $S$ depends only on the derivatives through order $N$ at zero. More explicitly, subtract from $g$ the function $$\eta(x)\sum_{|\alpha|\le N}
           \frac{\partial^\alpha g(0)}{\alpha!}x^\alpha.$$ The remainder has vanishing jet through order $N$ at zero, and therefore there are constants $c_\alpha\in\mathbb C$ such that $$S(g)=\sum_{|\alpha|\le N}c_\alpha\partial^\alpha g(0).$$ Using $T_v(g)=S(\widehat g)$ and differentiating the Fourier integral of a Schwartz function at zero gives $$T_v(g)=\int_{\mathbb R^n}P(x)g(x)\,dx,
 \qquad
 P(x)=\sum_{|\alpha|\le N}c_\alpha(-2\pi i x)^\alpha.$$ Every polynomially weighted Schwartz function is integrable, so the finite sum and these integrals may be interchanged. Since $v$ is locally integrable, equality on compact smooth tests implies $v=P$ almost everywhere.

Finally, <a href="#eq:rigidity-weighted-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rigidity-weighted-tail">[eq:rigidity-weighted-tail]</a> implies $$\int_{|x|\ge1}|P(x)|\,|x|^{-n-2}\,dx<\infty.$$ If $P$ had degree $k\ge2$, its nonzero leading homogeneous part would have absolute value bounded below on a nonempty open subset of the unit sphere. On the corresponding cone and for all sufficiently large $r$, $|P(r\theta)|\ge c r^k$. The last integral would then dominate a positive constant times $$\int_{R_0}^{\infty}r^{k-3}\,dr,$$ which diverges for $k\ge2$. (For $n=1$, one uses either of the two rays.) Hence $P$ has degree at most one. Taking real parts gives $v=b+\ell$ almost everywhere. If $|v|\le M$ almost everywhere, continuity gives $|b+\ell(x)|\le M$ for every $x$, since any open set violating this bound would have positive measure. Evaluating along rays shows $\ell=0$. ◻

</details>

The same conclusion holds if the compact weak equation is initially known only for real mean-zero tests: apply it separately to the real and imaginary parts, using the absolute integrability proved above.

<a id="sec:excess-propagation"></a>
### Relative excess improvement at every scale

The preceding section identifies the limiting normal heights as affine functions. We now turn that information into approximating planes. Two details matter: the heights are integrated against changing measures, and their normal coordinates belong to changing orthogonal frames. Polarization first gives strong convergence to fixed affine representatives. An explicit graph construction then gives planes with vanishing normalized excess. A contradiction argument makes this conclusion uniform in the measure, center, and scale.

<a id="eq:prop-upper"></a>
<a id="eq:prop-lower"></a>
<a id="eq:prop-riesz"></a>
Throughout this section, fix integers $1\le n<d$ and constants $c,G>0$ and $D_0\ge0$. We consider Radon measures $\sigma$ satisfying $$\begin{aligned}
 \sigma(B(x,s))&\le Gs^n
       &&(x\in\mathbb R^d,\ s>0),\label{eq:prop-upper}\\
 \sigma(B(x,s))&\ge cs^n
       &&(x\in\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\sigma,\ 0<s\le\mathop{\mathrm{diam}}(\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\sigma)),
       \label{eq:prop-lower}\\
 \|R_{\sigma,\eta}f\|_{L^2(\sigma)}
   &\le D_0\|f\|_{L^2(\sigma)}
       &&(\eta>0,\ f\in L^2(\sigma)).\label{eq:prop-riesz}
\end{aligned}$$ The last condition includes existence of the indicated $L^2$ output. All truncations and pairings are those defined in Section <a href="#sec:background" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:background">1</a>. The lower bound in <a href="#eq:prop-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prop-lower">[eq:prop-lower]</a> is required only at admissible radii; the upper bound in <a href="#eq:prop-upper" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prop-upper">[eq:prop-upper]</a> holds at every radius.

<a id="pm-h-30"></a>
#### Polarization under changing measures

The first lemma records exactly how the moment convergence from Lemma <a href="#lem:moving-compactness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:moving-compactness">19</a> becomes strong convergence. It uses an actual Lipschitz function as its target.

<a id="stmt-lem-affine-polarization"></a>
<div id="lem:affine-polarization" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:polarization-moment"></a>
**Lemma 22** (Polarization on a bounded region). *Let $\mu_j$ and $\nu$ be finite measures on $\mathbb R^d$, all concentrated on one bounded set, and suppose that $\mu_j$ converges weakly to $\nu$. Let $w_j\in L^2(\mu_j)$ and $f\in L^2(\nu)$. Suppose that <a id="eq:polarization-norm"></a>$$\label{eq:polarization-norm}
 \int w_j^2\,d\mu_j\longrightarrow\int f^2\,d\nu$$ and, for every compactly supported Lipschitz function $\psi$, $$\label{eq:polarization-moment}
 \int w_j\psi\,d\mu_j\longrightarrow\int f\psi\,d\nu.$$ If $v:\mathbb R^d\to\mathbb R$ is globally Lipschitz and $f=v$ almost everywhere for $\nu$, then $$\int|w_j-v|^2\,d\mu_j\longrightarrow0.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-affine-polarization"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-affine-polarization</summary>

*Proof.* Choose a compactly supported Lipschitz cutoff equal to one on a ball containing the common bounded set. Its product with $v$ is a compactly supported Lipschitz function $\widetilde v$ agreeing with $v$ on that set. Equation <a href="#eq:polarization-moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:polarization-moment">[eq:polarization-moment]</a>, with $\psi=\widetilde v$, gives $$\int w_jv\,d\mu_j\longrightarrow
 \int fv\,d\nu=\int f^2\,d\nu.$$ Weak convergence tested against the bounded continuous function $\widetilde v^2$ gives $$\int v^2\,d\mu_j\longrightarrow
 \int v^2\,d\nu=\int f^2\,d\nu.$$ The restrictions of $v$ belong to the respective $L^2$ spaces because they are bounded on the common set. Thus all terms in the identity $$\int|w_j-v|^2\,d\mu_j
 =\int w_j^2\,d\mu_j-2\int w_jv\,d\mu_j
       +\int v^2\,d\mu_j$$ are integrable. Their limits prove the assertion. ◻

</details>

Let $L:\mathbb R^n\to\mathbb R^d$ be the isometry onto the limiting plane. Use the regions $\Omega_m$ of <a href="#eq:height-projection-regions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:height-projection-regions">[eq:height-projection-regions]</a>, with coordinate map $L^*$. Each fixed ball lies in some $\Omega_m$. Lemma <a href="#lem:moving-compactness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:moving-compactness">19</a> supplies weak convergence of the finite restrictions and convergence of their signed and squared moments.

An intrinsic affine function $t\mapsto b+Mt$ on $L(\mathbb R^n)$ has the globally Lipschitz representative <a id="eq:ambient-affine-representative"></a>$$\label{eq:ambient-affine-representative}
 v(x)=b+ML^*x.$$ Thus Lemma <a href="#lem:affine-polarization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:affine-polarization">22</a> applies after affine rigidity. No representative of an arbitrary limiting $L^2$ class is evaluated against a different measure.

<a id="pm-h-31"></a>
#### Normal coordinates and tilted planes

<a id="eq:complete-normal-splitting"></a>
Write $q_0=d-n$. Let $a_j\in\mathbb R^d$, and let $L_j:\mathbb R^n\to\mathbb R^d$ and $N_j:\mathbb R^{q_0}\to\mathbb R^d$ be linear isometries forming a complete orthogonal splitting: $$\label{eq:complete-normal-splitting}
 L_j^*N_j=0,\qquad L_jL_j^*+N_jN_j^*=I_{\mathbb R^d}.$$ For positive numbers $\delta_j$, define the normalized normal height and the tangent projection by <a id="eq:normalized-vector-height"></a>$$\label{eq:normalized-vector-height}
 w_j(x)=\delta_j^{-1}N_j^*(x-a_j),\qquad
 P_jx=a_j+L_jL_j^*(x-a_j).$$

<a id="stmt-lem-normal-affine-planes"></a>
<div id="lem:normal-affine-planes" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:tilted-plane-distance-limit"></a>
**Lemma 23** (Affine heights produce affine planes). *Let $\mu_j$ be measures, let the frames satisfy <a href="#eq:complete-normal-splitting" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:complete-normal-splitting">[eq:complete-normal-splitting]</a>, and suppose that $\delta_j>0$ tends to zero. Assume $$\sup_j\int\|w_j\|^2\,d\mu_j<\infty$$ and that, for one fixed affine map $v(x)=b+Bx$ from $\mathbb R^d$ to $\mathbb R^{q_0}$, each function $\|w_j-v\|^2$ is integrable and $$\int\|w_j-v\|^2\,d\mu_j\longrightarrow0.$$ Then there are affine $n$-planes $S'_j$ such that $$\label{eq:tilted-plane-distance-limit}
 \int\left(\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(x,S'_j)}{\delta_j}\right)^2
       d\mu_j(x)\longrightarrow0.$$ Every integral in this conclusion is finite. No convergence of the normal frames $N_j$ is required.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-normal-affine-planes"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-normal-affine-planes</summary>

<a id="eq:physical-normal-error"></a>
<a id="eq:projected-affine-strong"></a>
*Proof.* The orthogonal decomposition gives $$\label{eq:physical-normal-error}
 x-a_j=L_jL_j^*(x-a_j)+\delta_jN_jw_j(x),
 \qquad |x-P_jx|=\delta_j\|w_j(x)\|.$$ Since $v$ has Lipschitz constant at most $\|B\|$, <a id="eq:affine-projection-replacement"></a>$$\label{eq:affine-projection-replacement}
 \int\|v(x)-v(P_jx)\|^2\,d\mu_j(x)
 \le\|B\|^2\delta_j^2\int\|w_j\|^2\,d\mu_j
 \longrightarrow0.$$ This estimate also proves integrability of the left side. The inequality $\|u+z\|^2\le2\|u\|^2+2\|z\|^2$ now yields $$\label{eq:projected-affine-strong}
 \int\|w_j(x)-v(P_jx)\|^2\,d\mu_j(x)\longrightarrow0.$$

<a id="eq:genuine-tilted-plane"></a>
Set $c_j=b+Ba_j$, $A_j'=BL_j$, and define $$\label{eq:genuine-tilted-plane}
 S'_j=\bigl\{a_j+L_jt+\delta_jN_j(c_j+A_j't):t\in\mathbb R^n\bigr\}.$$ Its direction is the range of $L_j+\delta_jN_jA_j'$. By <a href="#eq:complete-normal-splitting" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:complete-normal-splitting">[eq:complete-normal-splitting]</a>, $$L_j^*(L_j+\delta_jN_jA_j')=I_{\mathbb R^n}.$$ The parametrizing linear map is therefore injective. Its range has dimension $n$, and <a href="#eq:genuine-tilted-plane" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:genuine-tilted-plane">[eq:genuine-tilted-plane]</a> is a nonempty affine $n$-plane. This conclusion needs no bound on its slope.

For a given $x$, choose $t=L_j^*(x-a_j)$ in <a href="#eq:genuine-tilted-plane" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:genuine-tilted-plane">[eq:genuine-tilted-plane]</a>. Since $v(P_jx)=c_j+A_j't$, <a href="#eq:physical-normal-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:physical-normal-error">[eq:physical-normal-error]</a> gives $$\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(x,S'_j)}{\delta_j}
 \le\|w_j(x)-v(P_jx)\|.$$ The squared left side is measurable and dominated by an integrable function. Integrating and using <a href="#eq:projected-affine-strong" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:projected-affine-strong">[eq:projected-affine-strong]</a> proves <a href="#eq:tilted-plane-distance-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tilted-plane-distance-limit">[eq:tilted-plane-distance-limit]</a>. ◻

</details>

Lemma <a href="#lem:moving-compactness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:moving-compactness">19</a> treats the finite family of normal coordinates on one common subsequence. Proposition <a href="#prop:height-equation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:height-equation">20</a> and Lemma <a href="#lem:fractional-rigidity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:fractional-rigidity">21</a> identify those same limiting functions without further extraction.

<a id="pm-h-32"></a>
#### The sequential improvement

<a id="stmt-prop-normalized-excess-limit"></a>
<div id="prop:normalized-excess-limit" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:sequential-horizon-excess"></a>
<a id="eq:sequential-excess-conclusion"></a>
**Proposition 24** (Vanishing normalized excess). *Fix $0<\gamma<1/2$. Let $\mu_j$ satisfy <a href="#eq:prop-upper" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prop-upper">[eq:prop-upper]</a>–<a href="#eq:prop-riesz" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prop-riesz">[eq:prop-riesz]</a> with the same constants, and suppose that $0\in\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\mu_j$. Let $\delta_j>0$ and $K_j\in\mathbb N$ satisfy <a id="eq:sequential-horizon-schedule"></a>$$\label{eq:sequential-horizon-schedule}
 \delta_j\to0,\qquad K_j\to\infty,\qquad
 \delta_j2^{\gamma K_j}\to0,\qquad
 \delta_j^{-1}2^{-K_j}\to0.$$ Assume that $2^{K_j}\le\mathop{\mathrm{diam}}(\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\mu_j)$ and $$\label{eq:sequential-horizon-excess}
 e_{\mu_j}(0,2^\ell)\le\delta_j2^{\gamma\ell}
       \qquad(0\le\ell\le K_j).$$ Put $A_j=8\,2^{K_j}$. Suppose, for every compactly supported 1-Lipschitz scalar function $\varphi$ with $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\varphi\subset B(0,A_j)$ and $\int\varphi\,d\mu_j=0$, that <a id="eq:sequential-cubic-oscillation"></a>$$\label{eq:sequential-cubic-oscillation}
 |\mathcal R_{\mu_j}(\varphi)|\le\delta_j^3.$$ There is one subsequence along which, for every fixed $r>0$, $$\label{eq:sequential-excess-conclusion}
 \frac{e_{\mu_j}(0,r)}{\delta_j}\longrightarrow0,
 \qquad b_{\mu_j}(0,r)\longrightarrow0.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-normalized-excess-limit"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-normalized-excess-limit</summary>

*Proof.* Admissibility of the growing horizons implies $\mathop{\mathrm{diam}}(\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\mu_j)\to\infty$. Lemma <a href="#lem:plane-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:plane-comparison">14</a> and Proposition <a href="#prop:flat-limit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:flat-limit">17</a>, applied to <a href="#eq:sequential-horizon-excess" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sequential-horizon-excess">[eq:sequential-horizon-excess]</a>, give, after discarding finitely many indices and passing to a subsequence, a full constant-density plane limit $$\mu_j\rightharpoonup\nu=q\mathcal H^n|_{L(\mathbb R^n)},\qquad q>0,$$ where $L$ is a linear isometry. They also give approximate fitting planes $S_j=a_j+L_j(\mathbb R^n)$, with $a_j\to0$ and $L_j\to L$, and fixed-plane moment bounds <a id="eq:prop-fixed-plane-moments"></a>$$\label{eq:prop-fixed-plane-moments}
 \int_{B(0,2^\ell)}\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(x,S_j)^2\,d\mu_j(x)
 \le C_*\delta_j^2(2^\ell)^{n+2+2\gamma}
       \qquad(0\le\ell\le K_j).$$ The constant $C_*$ is independent of $j$ and $\ell$. The fitting planes are approximate minimizers; no existence of an exact minimizer is used.

Choose complementary normal isometries $N_j$ so that <a href="#eq:complete-normal-splitting" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:complete-normal-splitting">[eq:complete-normal-splitting]</a> holds. For $i=1,\dots,q_0$, write $$u_{j,i}(x)=\langle N_je_i,x-a_j\rangle,
 \qquad w_{j,i}(x)=\delta_j^{-1}u_{j,i}(x),$$ where $(e_i)$ is the standard orthonormal basis of $\mathbb R^{q_0}$. The identity $\sum_i u_{j,i}(x)^2=\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(x,S_j)^2$ supplies the normal-coordinate moment bounds required by Lemma <a href="#lem:height-energy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:height-energy">18</a>. That lemma and Lemma <a href="#lem:moving-compactness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:moving-compactness">19</a>, with a common subsequence as described above, give measurable limiting heights $f_i$ on the limiting plane. On every region $\Omega_m$, they satisfy <a id="eq:normal-coordinate-norm-limit"></a><a id="eq:normal-coordinate-signed-limit"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \int_{\Omega_m}w_{j,i}^2\,d\mu_j
   &\longrightarrow\int_{\Omega_m}f_i^2\,d\nu,
       \label{eq:normal-coordinate-norm-limit}\\
 \int_{\Omega_m}w_{j,i}\psi\,d\mu_j
   &\longrightarrow\int_{\Omega_m}f_i\psi\,d\nu
       \label{eq:normal-coordinate-signed-limit}
\end{aligned}$$ for every compactly supported Lipschitz $\psi$. All these statements concern the same heights and the same subsequence.

<a id="eq:vector-affine-strong-limit"></a>
Proposition <a href="#prop:height-equation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:height-equation">20</a> gives the mean-zero fractional equation and the weighted tail integrability of each $f_i$. Lemma <a href="#lem:fractional-rigidity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:fractional-rigidity">21</a> therefore gives a scalar $b_i$ and a linear functional $M_i$ on $\mathbb R^n$ such that $$f_i(Lt)=b_i+M_it\quad\text{for almost every }t\in\mathbb R^n.$$ Use the ambient affine representative $v_i(x)=b_i+M_iL^*x$. Applying Lemma <a href="#lem:affine-polarization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:affine-polarization">22</a> to the finite restrictions on $\Omega_m$ yields $$\int_{\Omega_m}|w_{j,i}-v_i|^2\,d\mu_j\longrightarrow0.$$ Assemble $v=(v_1,\dots,v_{q_0})=b+Bx$. The Euclidean squared norm is the sum of the squared coordinates, so $$\label{eq:vector-affine-strong-limit}
 \int_{\Omega_m}\|w_j-v\|^2\,d\mu_j\longrightarrow0,
 \qquad \sup_j\int_{\Omega_m}\|w_j\|^2\,d\mu_j<\infty.$$ Integrability of each coordinate square justifies the finite summation.

Fix $r>0$ and choose $m$ with $B(0,r)\subset\Omega_m$. The integrands in <a href="#eq:vector-affine-strong-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:vector-affine-strong-limit">[eq:vector-affine-strong-limit]</a> are nonnegative. Restricting them to $B(0,r)$ preserves the uniform bound and the zero limit. In particular, this step requires no assertion about mass on the sphere $\partial B(0,r)$. Lemma <a href="#lem:normal-affine-planes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:normal-affine-planes">23</a>, applied to the restrictions on this ball, supplies affine $n$-planes $S'_j$ with $$\int_{B(0,r)}\left(\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(x,S'_j)}{\delta_j}\right)^2
       d\mu_j(x)\longrightarrow0.$$ By the definition of the excess, $$0\le\left(\frac{e_{\mu_j}(0,r)}{\delta_j}\right)^2
 \le r^{-n-2}\int_{B(0,r)}
       \left(\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(x,S'_j)}{\delta_j}\right)^2d\mu_j(x).$$ This proves the first limit in <a href="#eq:sequential-excess-conclusion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sequential-excess-conclusion">[eq:sequential-excess-conclusion]</a> for every fixed $r$, without another extraction.

Proposition <a href="#prop:flat-limit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:flat-limit">17</a> also gives bilateral convergence of the supports to $L(\mathbb R^n)$ on every bounded ball. This convergence persists on the subsequence used for the height limits. For every fixed $r>0$, both directed deviations in the definition of $b_{\mu_j}(0,r)$ therefore tend to zero when evaluated at the plane $L(\mathbb R^n)$. Their sum divided by $r$ bounds $b_{\mu_j}(0,r)$ from above, which proves the second limit in <a href="#eq:sequential-excess-conclusion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sequential-excess-conclusion">[eq:sequential-excess-conclusion]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-33"></a>
#### Uniform propagation at every admissible scale

The next theorem is the input used in the cell-packing argument. The normalization of its tests agrees with that of $W_J(Q)$ after replacing the cell scale by $r$.

<a id="stmt-thm-excess-propagation"></a>
<div id="thm:excess-propagation" class="theorem">

<a id="eq:prop-oscillation-assumption"></a>
<a id="eq:uniform-propagation-conclusion"></a>
**Theorem 25** (Uniform excess propagation). *Fix $1\le n<d$, $c,G>0$, $D_0\ge0$, $0<\gamma<1/2$, and $\varepsilon>0$. For every integer $J_0\ge0$, there is an integer $J\ge J_0$, depending only on these fixed data, with the following property. Set <a id="eq:uniform-propagation-constants"></a>$$\label{eq:uniform-propagation-constants}
 \delta=2^{-J},\qquad K=2J+3,\qquad A=8\,2^K.$$ Let $\mu$ satisfy <a href="#eq:prop-upper" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prop-upper">[eq:prop-upper]</a>–<a href="#eq:prop-riesz" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prop-riesz">[eq:prop-riesz]</a>, let $a\in\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\mu$, and let $r>0$. Assume <a id="eq:prop-admissible-horizon"></a><a id="eq:prop-horizon-assumption"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 2^Kr&\le\mathop{\mathrm{diam}}(\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\mu),\label{eq:prop-admissible-horizon}\\
 e_\mu(a,2^\ell r)&\le\delta2^{\gamma\ell}
       &&(0\le\ell\le K),\label{eq:prop-horizon-assumption}
\end{aligned}$$ and assume $$\label{eq:prop-oscillation-assumption}
 |\mathcal R_\mu(\varphi)|\le\delta^3r^n$$ for every compactly supported scalar function $\varphi$ satisfying $$\mathop{\mathrm{Lip}}(\varphi)\le r^{-1},\qquad
 \mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\varphi\subset B(a,Ar),\qquad
 \int\varphi\,d\mu=0.$$ Then $$\label{eq:uniform-propagation-conclusion}
 e_\mu(a,r/2)\le\delta,\qquad
 b_\mu(a,r/2)<\varepsilon.$$ Equivalently, the oscillation hypothesis can be stated with $\mathop{\mathrm{Lip}}(\varphi)\le1$ and threshold $\delta^3r^{n+1}$. The constants $J,\delta,K,A$ are chosen before the measure, center, and radius. The test radius $Ar$ need not be admissible.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-thm-excess-propagation"><summary>Proof of stmt-thm-excess-propagation</summary>

*Proof.* We first work at center zero and scale one. If the assertion failed, then for each $j\ge0$ we could choose a measure $\mu_j$ satisfying the hypotheses at the index $j+J_0$, for which at least one conclusion in <a href="#eq:uniform-propagation-conclusion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:uniform-propagation-conclusion">[eq:uniform-propagation-conclusion]</a> fails. Thus, with $$\delta_j=2^{-(j+J_0)},\qquad
 K_j=2(j+J_0)+3,\qquad A_j=8\,2^{K_j},$$ we would have either $e_{\mu_j}(0,1/2)>\delta_j$ or $b_{\mu_j}(0,1/2)\ge\varepsilon$ at every index. The constants in the growth and operator hypotheses remain $c,G,D_0$ for this entire sequence. Moreover, $$\begin{aligned}
 \delta_j2^{\gamma K_j}
   &=2^{3\gamma}\,2^{-(1-2\gamma)(j+J_0)}\longrightarrow0,\\
 \delta_j^{-1}2^{-K_j}
   &=2^{-(j+J_0)-3}\longrightarrow0.
\end{aligned}$$ Thus every hypothesis of Proposition <a href="#prop:normalized-excess-limit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:normalized-excess-limit">24</a> holds. On its common subsequence, the excess divided by $\delta_j$ is eventually less than one and the bilateral beta number is eventually less than $\varepsilon$. This contradicts the selected failure of their conjunction. We have obtained one $J\ge J_0$ that works for every source measure at center zero and scale one.

<a id="eq:prop-flatness-covariance"></a>
Fix this $J$. For arbitrary $\mu,a,r$ in the statement, define the normalized measure $$\sigma=r^{-n}(T_{a,r})_\#\mu,
 \qquad T_{a,r}(x)=\frac{x-a}{r}.$$ The upper and admissible lower growth constants are unchanged, the origin belongs to its support, and $2^K\le\mathop{\mathrm{diam}}(\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\sigma)$. The conjugation of hard truncations under $T_{a,r}$ preserves the bound $D_0$; the truncation parameter $\eta$ for $\sigma$ corresponds exactly to $r\eta$ for $\mu$. Transporting all affine $n$-planes by this affine bijection gives $$\label{eq:prop-flatness-covariance}
 e_\sigma(0,s)=e_\mu(a,rs),\qquad
 b_\sigma(0,s)=b_\mu(a,rs).$$ For the first equality, the squared distance contributes $r^{-2}$, the measure contributes $r^{-n}$, and the normalizing radius contributes $s^{-n-2}$; the result is exactly the expression normalized by $(rs)^{n+2}$. The second equality follows directly by scaling both directed distances and the radius by $r$.

If $\psi$ is a compactly supported 1-Lipschitz test in $B(0,A)$ with zero $\sigma$-mean, then $\varphi(x)=\psi(T_{a,r}x)$ has Lipschitz constant at most $r^{-1}$, is supported in $B(a,Ar)$, and has zero $\mu$-mean. The homogeneity of the kernel, applied to the renormalized pairing of Lemma <a href="#lem:pairings" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:pairings">5</a>, gives $$\mathcal R_\sigma(\psi)=r^{-n}\mathcal R_\mu(\varphi).$$ Hence <a href="#eq:prop-oscillation-assumption" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prop-oscillation-assumption">[eq:prop-oscillation-assumption]</a> supplies the normalized bound $|\mathcal R_\sigma(\psi)|\le\delta^3$. The scale-one assertion applies to $\sigma$, and <a href="#eq:prop-flatness-covariance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prop-flatness-covariance">[eq:prop-flatness-covariance]</a> gives <a href="#eq:uniform-propagation-conclusion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:uniform-propagation-conclusion">[eq:uniform-propagation-conclusion]</a> for $\mu$. Finally, multiplying or dividing a test by $r$ proves the equivalence of the two oscillation normalizations. ◻

</details>

We henceforth fix $\gamma=1/4$. The smallness parameter in Theorem <a href="#thm:excess-propagation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:excess-propagation">25</a> can be made smaller than any prescribed positive constant by increasing $J_0$ before choosing $J$. The conclusion is simultaneous in excess and bilateral flatness; there is no need to reconcile separately selected thresholds.

<a id="sec:packing-endpoint"></a>
### Packing failures and the Lipschitz-image conclusion

We now combine the two exceptional-family estimates with excess propagation. A flat descendant starts the propagation; a later failure of bilateral flatness forces an oscillatory ancestor. The finite counting argument below converts this implication into a Carleson estimate without requiring selected flat descendants to be disjoint.

Throughout this section, $\mu$ satisfies the hypotheses of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">3</a>, $E=\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\mu$ has positive diameter, and $\mathcal D$ is the lattice of Proposition <a href="#prop:dyadic-lattice" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:dyadic-lattice">8</a>. Write $\mathcal D(R)=\{Q\in\mathcal D:Q\subset R\}$ and let $k(Q)$ be the generation of $Q$, so that $\ell(Q)=2^{-k(Q)}$. Fix $$H\geq 4(C_0+1),\qquad
 \beta_H(Q)=b_\mu(z_Q,H\ell(Q)).$$ All constants below are uniform in the support center and scale. Their permitted dependencies are $d,n,C_{\rm AD},C_{\rm R}$ and the explicitly chosen apertures and error thresholds.

<a id="pm-h-35"></a>
#### A finite seed-counting lemma

The next lemma concerns only a nested measurable partition. Its role is to count parents by the mass of one selected descendant of each parent.

<a id="stmt-lem-seed-charging"></a>
<div id="lem:seed-charging" class="lemma">

**Lemma 26** (Finite seed charging). *Let $R\in\mathcal D$ and let $\mathcal F$ be a finite family of subcubes of $R$. For each $Q\in\mathcal F$, choose a cube $S(Q)\subset Q$ such that, for fixed $N\in\mathbb N$ and $M\geq1$, $$k(Q)\leq k(S(Q))\leq k(Q)+N,
 \qquad \mu(Q)\leq M\mu(S(Q)).$$ Let $\mathcal O\subset\mathcal D(R)$ satisfy $\sum_{T\in\mathcal O}\mu(T)\leq K\mu(R)$. Suppose that whenever $Q,Q'\in\mathcal F$ satisfy $$k(S(Q))<k(Q'),\qquad Q'\subset S(Q),$$ there is a cube $T\in\mathcal O$ with $$k(S(Q))\leq k(T)<k(Q'),\qquad Q'\subset T.$$ Then $$\sum_{Q\in\mathcal F}\mu(Q)
 \leq M(N+1)(1+K)\mu(R).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-seed-charging"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-seed-charging</summary>

*Proof.* For every eligible ordered pair $(Q,Q')$, choose one such $T$, and let $\mathcal O_0$ be the resulting finite set. Make these choices before fixing a point. Outside the common null set of lattice boundaries, fix $x\in R$ and list the parents whose seeds contain $x$ in increasing order of generation. Their generations are distinct, because their parents also contain $x$.

Starting with the first parent $Q_1$, group together every listed parent of generation at most $k(S(Q_1))$. This group has at most $N+1$ members. If another parent remains, let $Q_2$ be the first one. Nesting gives $Q_2\subset S(Q_1)$, so the chosen charge for $(Q_1,Q_2)$ contains $x$ and has generation in $[k(S(Q_1)),k(Q_2))$. Repeat this procedure. The intervals supplying successive charges are disjoint: the next interval starts at $k(S(Q_2))\geq k(Q_2)$. Thus the number of groups is at most $1+\sum_{T\in\mathcal O_0}\mathbf1_T(x)$, and $$\sum_{Q\in\mathcal F}\mathbf1_{S(Q)}(x)
 \leq (N+1)\left(\mathbf1_R(x)
               +\sum_{T\in\mathcal O_0}\mathbf1_T(x)\right).$$ Integrating this finite inequality and using the two mass bounds proves the result. In particular, overlapping seeds and arbitrarily long gaps between selected generations cause no difficulty. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-36"></a>
#### Propagation along a cube chain

<a id="stmt-prop-beta-packing"></a>
<div id="prop:beta-packing" class="proposition">

**Proposition 27** (Bilateral flatness has Carleson failures). *For each $H\geq4(C_0+1)$ and $\varepsilon>0$, there is a constant $C_{H,\varepsilon}<\infty$, depending only on $d,n,C_{\rm AD},C_{\rm R},H,\varepsilon$, such that $$\sum_{\substack{Q\subset R\\\beta_H(Q)\geq\varepsilon}}\mu(Q)
 \leq C_{H,\varepsilon}\mu(R)
 \qquad(R\in\mathcal D).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-beta-packing"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-beta-packing</summary>

<a id="eq:seed-plane-bounds"></a>
<a id="eq:seed-mass-comparison"></a>
*Proof.* We first fix all numerical parameters. Apply Theorem <a href="#thm:excess-propagation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:excess-propagation">25</a> with $\gamma=1/4$ and output tolerance $\varepsilon$. Write its resulting constants as $$\delta=2^{-j_*},\qquad K_*=2j_*+3,\qquad A_*=8\cdot2^{K_*}.$$ Choose $$A_{\rm f}>H2^{K_*}+2C_0+1,\qquad
 0<\alpha<\min\left\{\frac{\varepsilon H}{4},
                  \frac{\delta H}{2\sqrt{G+1}}\right\},
 \qquad J_{\rm o}>A_*H+2C_0+1,
 \qquad v=\frac{\delta^3}{2}.$$ Here $G\leq9^dC_{\rm AD}$ is supplied by Lemma <a href="#lem:global-growth" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:global-growth">4</a>. Proposition <a href="#prop:oscillation-packing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:oscillation-packing">10</a> shows that $$\mathcal O=\{T\in\mathcal D:
                  W_{J_{\rm o}}(T)>v\ell(T)^n\}$$ is Carleson; denote its constant by $C_{\rm o}$. Proposition <a href="#prop:flat-seeds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:flat-seeds">11</a>, with aperture $A_{\rm f}$ and tolerance $\alpha/(2A_{\rm f})$, supplies a Carleson family $\mathcal C$, a fixed depth $N$, and, for every $Q\notin\mathcal C$, a descendant $S(Q)\subset Q$ at depth at most $N$ with $$b_\mu(z_{S(Q)},A_{\rm f}\ell(S(Q)))
       <\frac{\alpha}{2A_{\rm f}}.$$ A near-minimizing plane $L_S$ consequently satisfies both directed bounds $$\label{eq:seed-plane-bounds}
 \sup_{E\cap B(z_S,A_{\rm f}\ell(S))}\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(\cdot,L_S)
 \leq\alpha\ell(S),\qquad
 \sup_{L_S\cap B(z_S,A_{\rm f}\ell(S))}\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(\cdot,E)
 \leq\alpha\ell(S).$$ The lattice mass bounds give a uniform $M\geq1$ such that $$\label{eq:seed-mass-comparison}
 \mu(Q)\leq M\mu(S(Q)),\qquad
 M\leq C(d,n,C_{\rm AD})2^{nN}.$$

Before iterating propagation, we ensure its admissible horizon. The required condition is $2^{K_*}H\ell(S)\leq D_E$. If $D_E<\infty$, restrict for now to parents satisfying $\ell(Q)\leq D_E/B_*$, where $B_*=H2^{K_*}$. Every selected seed then satisfies the condition, as do all later radii in its chain. If $D_E=\infty$, there is no cutoff. This restriction costs only finitely many generations: the largest lattice scale lies in $[D_E,2D_E)$, so there are at most $2+\lceil\log_2 B_*\rceil$ omitted generations. At each generation, subcubes of a given $R$ are disjoint and have total mass at most $\mu(R)$. We will restore all omitted cubes at this fixed Carleson cost.

We prove the implication needed by Lemma <a href="#lem:seed-charging" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:seed-charging">26</a>. Let $S$ be one of these flat seeds and let $Q'\subset S$ be a proper descendant, with $m=k(Q')-k(S)>0$. Suppose that every proper ancestor of $Q'$ between $S$ and $Q'$ lies outside $\mathcal O$. We claim that $\beta_H(Q')<\varepsilon$.

<a id="eq:seed-initial-excess"></a>
Keep the center $a=z_{Q'}$ fixed throughout the argument. Let $T_j$ be the ancestor of $Q'$ of generation $k(S)+j$, and set $$r_j=H2^{-j}\ell(S)=H\ell(T_j),\qquad 0\leq j\leq m.$$ The center belongs to the closure of every $T_j$, so $|a-z_{T_j}|\leq2C_0\ell(T_j)$. In particular, $B(a,2^{K_*}r_0)\subset B(z_S,A_{\rm f}\ell(S))$. The first bound in <a href="#eq:seed-plane-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:seed-plane-bounds">[eq:seed-plane-bounds]</a> and global upper growth give, for $0\leq i\leq K_*$, $$\label{eq:seed-initial-excess}
 e_\mu(a,2^ir_0)
 \leq\frac{\sqrt G\,\alpha\ell(S)}{2^ir_0}
 \leq\delta\,2^{i/4}.$$

<a id="eq:chain-oscillation"></a>
For $j<m$, a compactly supported mean-zero test $\varphi$ with $\mathop{\mathrm{Lip}}\varphi\leq r_j^{-1}$ and $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\varphi\subset B(a,A_*r_j)$ is admissible in the definition of $W_{J_{\rm o}}(T_j)$: its support lies in the required enlarged ball, and $r_j\geq\ell(T_j)$. Therefore $$\label{eq:chain-oscillation}
 |\mathcal R_\mu(\varphi)|
 \leq v\ell(T_j)^n\leq\delta^3r_j^n.$$ We may now iterate Theorem <a href="#thm:excess-propagation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:excess-propagation">25</a> at the radii $r_0,r_1,\ldots,r_{m-1}$. To check the complete horizon at the $j$th step, observe that $$2^ir_j=
 \begin{cases}
 r_{j-i},&i\leq j,\\
 2^{i-j}r_0,&i>j.
 \end{cases}$$ In the first case the preceding induction gives excess at most $\delta$; in the second, <a href="#eq:seed-initial-excess" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:seed-initial-excess">[eq:seed-initial-excess]</a> gives at most $\delta2^{(i-j)/4}\leq\delta2^{i/4}$. Thus all horizon inequalities hold at every step. Together with <a href="#eq:chain-oscillation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:chain-oscillation">[eq:chain-oscillation]</a>, they prove $$e_\mu(a,r_j)\leq\delta\quad(0\leq j\leq m),\qquad
 b_\mu(a,r_j)<\varepsilon\quad(1\leq j\leq m).$$ Since $r_m=H\ell(Q')$, the claim follows. Notice that no test at the terminal cube $Q'$ was used.

Fix $R\in\mathcal D$. Take any finite family $\mathcal F$ of remaining subcubes of $R$ with $\beta_H(Q)\geq\varepsilon$, and remove the members of $\mathcal C$. For each remaining parent use the seed selected above. If $Q'\subset S(Q)$ and $k(Q')>k(S(Q))$, then the just-proved implication, applied contrapositively, supplies $T\in\mathcal O$ with $$S(Q)\supset T\supset Q',\qquad
 k(S(Q))\leq k(T)<k(Q').$$ Lemma <a href="#lem:seed-charging" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:seed-charging">26</a> and <a href="#eq:seed-mass-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:seed-mass-comparison">[eq:seed-mass-comparison]</a> give $$\sum_{Q\in\mathcal F\setminus\mathcal C}\mu(Q)
 \leq M(N+1)(1+C_{\rm o})\mu(R).$$ Add the Carleson bound for $\mathcal C$ and the finite coarse-generation cost. Taking the supremum over finite families proves the asserted countable sum. Every parameter was fixed before $R$ or any seed was chosen. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-37"></a>
#### From dyadic packing to the bilateral weak geometric lemma

We spell out the passage to the intrinsic ball condition, so the final geometric theorem does not depend on a choice of lattice. First, $b_\mu$ is Borel on $E\times(0,\infty)$. Indeed, the two unnormalized directed deviations on an open ball are jointly lower semicontinuous in its center, radius, and affine plane. Points strictly inside a ball persist under small perturbations, and points of a limiting plane can be approximated by points of the varying planes. Along a convergent sequence of centers and positive radii, near-minimizing planes with bounded error have bounded offsets: the center belongs to $E$ and its distance to the plane is one of the first directed deviations. Compactness of the Grassmannian then supplies a convergent plane subsequence. The two lower-semicontinuity inequalities show that their infimum $b_\mu$ is lower semicontinuous as well.

<a id="eq:continuous-bwgl"></a>
Fix $\varepsilon>0$, $a\in E$, and $0<R<D_E$. Choose a lattice scale $s$ with $R\leq s<2R$, and put $s_j=2^{-j}s$. For almost every $x$, let $Q_j(x)$ be its cube of side scale $s_j$. Whenever $s_j/2<t\leq s_j$, $$B(x,t)\subset B(z_{Q_j(x)},Hs_j),\qquad
 b_\mu(x,t)\leq\frac{Hs_j}{t}\beta_H(Q_j(x))
                  \leq2H\beta_H(Q_j(x)).$$ Both inequalities follow directly from the two directed deviations; the same plane is used before taking the infimum. Integrating over the dyadic scale intervals and then over $E\cap B(a,R)$ yields $$\begin{aligned}
 &\int_{E\cap B(a,R)}\int_0^R
       \mathbf1_{\{b_\mu(x,t)>\varepsilon\}}\,\frac{dt}{t}\,d\mu(x)\\
 &\qquad\leq(\log2)
   \sum_{\substack{Q\text{ at scale }s_j,\ j\geq0\\
          Q\cap B(a,R)\ne\varnothing\\
          \beta_H(Q)>\varepsilon/(2H)}}\mu(Q).
\end{aligned}$$ Group these cubes by their scale-$s$ ancestors $P$. Such a $P$ meets $B(a,R)$ and is contained in $B(a,(1+4C_0)R)$. Applying Proposition <a href="#prop:beta-packing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:beta-packing">27</a> to each $P$, then summing their disjoint masses and using global growth, proves $$\label{eq:continuous-bwgl}
 \int_{E\cap B(a,R)}\int_0^R
       \mathbf1_{\{b_\mu(x,t)>\varepsilon\}}\,\frac{dt}{t}\,d\mu(x)
 \leq C_\varepsilon R^n.$$ This is the *bilateral weak geometric lemma*: for every positive flatness threshold, its failures occupy a Carleson set of positions and scales.

<a id="pm-h-38"></a>
#### The geometric criterion and the ball-map conclusion

<a id="eq:measure-hausdorff-comparison"></a>
For clarity, we recall the measure comparison used to apply the geometric criterion. AD regularity with $D_E>0$ gives $$\label{eq:measure-hausdorff-comparison}
 c\,\mathcal H^n\!\restriction E\ \leq\ \mu\ \leq\
 C\,\mathcal H^n\!\restriction E,$$ with positive finite constants depending only on the AD data and the Hausdorff-measure normalization. Here is a direct justification. Cover a subset of $E$ by sets of arbitrarily small diameter, recenter each nonempty set at a point of $E$, and use the upper ball bound. Infimizing the cover sums proves the upper inequality. For the reverse inequality, let $F\subset E$ be bounded and Borel, and let $U$ be an open set containing $F$. Cover $F$ by support-centered balls contained in $U$, of radii less than both $D_E/10$ and an arbitrarily small prescribed number. The $5r$ covering lemma selects disjoint balls $B_i$ whose fivefold dilates cover $F$. The lower AD bound gives $$\mathcal H^n_\eta(F)\leq C_n\sum_i r_i^n
      \leq C_nC_{\rm AD}\sum_i\mu(B_i)
      \leq C_nC_{\rm AD}\mu(U),$$ Here $\mathcal H^n_\eta$ denotes Hausdorff content using covers of diameter less than $\eta$, with the same normalization as $\mathcal H^n$; the chosen radii make all fivefold diameters less than $\eta$. Let $\eta\downarrow0$, use outer regularity, and then exhaust unbounded sets by bounded sets. This proves <a href="#eq:measure-hausdorff-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:measure-hausdorff-comparison">[eq:measure-hausdorff-comparison]</a>. In particular $E$ is an $n$-AD-regular set for $\mathcal H^n$. Write $b_E$ for the bilateral coefficient of Definition <a href="#def:flatness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="def:flatness">7</a> with support set $E$, so that $b_E=b_\mu$. Replacing $d\mu$ by $d\mathcal H^n$ in <a href="#eq:continuous-bwgl" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:continuous-bwgl">[eq:continuous-bwgl]</a> changes only its constant.

<a id="stmt-thm-bwgl"></a>
<div id="thm:bwgl" class="theorem">

**Theorem 28** (David–Semmes bilateral criterion). *Let $E\subset\mathbb R^d$ be closed, of positive diameter, and $n$-AD regular with respect to $\mathcal H^n$. Suppose that for every $\varepsilon>0$ there is $C_\varepsilon<\infty$ such that $$\int_{E\cap B(a,R)}\int_0^R
   \mathbf1_{\{b_E(x,t)>\varepsilon\}}\,\frac{dt}{t}\,d\mathcal H^n(x)
 \leq C_\varepsilon R^n
 \quad(a\in E,\ 0<R<\mathop{\mathrm{diam}}E).$$ Then $E$ has big pieces of uniformly Lipschitz images: there are $\theta_0>0$ and $L<\infty$ such that for every $a\in E$ and $0<r<\mathop{\mathrm{diam}}E$ there is an $L$-Lipschitz map $f:B^n(0,r)\to\mathbb R^d$ for which $$\mathcal H^n\bigl(E\cap B(a,r)\cap f(B^n(0,r))\bigr)\geq\theta_0r^n.$$ The constants depend only on the dimensions, AD constants, and the constants in the bilateral weak geometric lemma.*

</div>

This is the Euclidean David–Semmes criterion (David and Semmes 1993, vol. 38, chap. II.2) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[DavidSemmes1993](#ref-DavidSemmes1993)]; see also (Tolsa 2015, Theorem 2.5) <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[Tolsa2015Uniform](#ref-Tolsa2015Uniform)] for the all-scale formulation and (Bate, Hyde, and Schul 2023, Theorem 1.0.4) <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[BateHydeSchul2023](#ref-BateHydeSchul2023)] for finite diameter. Appendix <a href="#app:geometric-conventions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:geometric-conventions">9</a> verifies the finite-diameter, open-ball and positive-mass conventions needed for this formulation.

<details id="proof-stmt-thm-bwgl"><summary>Proof of stmt-thm-bwgl</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">3</a>.* If $E$ is empty or a singleton, there is no positive admissible radius, and the asserted ball-map conclusion is vacuous. Otherwise Proposition <a href="#prop:beta-packing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:beta-packing">27</a> gives <a href="#eq:continuous-bwgl" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:continuous-bwgl">[eq:continuous-bwgl]</a> for every threshold. The comparison <a href="#eq:measure-hausdorff-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:measure-hausdorff-comparison">[eq:measure-hausdorff-comparison]</a> permits application of Theorem <a href="#thm:bwgl" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:bwgl">28</a>; its positive image-mass bound also holds with $\mu$, after changing the positive constant.

For any $a\in E$ and $0<r\leq D_E$, apply this conclusion at $r/2$ and write its map as $f:B^n(0,r/2)\to\mathbb R^d$. The map $$g:B^n(0,r)\longrightarrow\mathbb R^d,\qquad g(u)=f(u/2),$$ has the same image and Lipschitz constant at most $L/2$. Since $B(a,r/2)\subset B(a,r)$, it captures at least $c\theta_0 2^{-n}r^n$ of the original measure in $B(a,r)$. This also covers $r=D_E$ when the diameter is finite. All constants were chosen before $a$ and $r$, and have only the dependencies asserted in the theorem. Thus the uniform ball-map condition in Definition <a href="#def:ur" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="def:ur">2</a> holds. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:consequences"></a>
### Variation and principal values

<a id="eq:variation-kernel"></a>
<a id="eq:variation-cutoff"></a>
The uniform-rectifiability conclusion also allows the forward estimates of Mas and Tolsa to be applied under the individual hard-truncation bound <a href="#eq:riesz-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:riesz-bound">[eq:riesz-bound]</a>. For a scalar or Euclidean-vector family $F=(F_\varepsilon)_{\varepsilon>0}$ and $\rho>2$, write $$V_\rho(F)(x)=
 \sup_{\substack{m\ge1\\ \varepsilon_0>\cdots>\varepsilon_m>0}}
 \left(\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}
 |F_{\varepsilon_{j+1}}(x)-F_{\varepsilon_j}(x)|^\rho
 \right)^{1/\rho}.$$ Thus the supremum is over all finite decreasing sequences of positive scales, with the Euclidean norm for vector values. Let $K:\mathbb R^d\setminus\{0\}\to\mathbb R$ be an odd $C^2$ convolution kernel satisfying, for some finite $C_K>0$, $$\label{eq:variation-kernel}
 K(-z)=-K(z),\qquad
 |\partial^\beta K(z)|\le C_K|z|^{-n-|\beta|}
 \quad (|\beta|\le2,\ z\ne0),$$ and set $$T^{K,\mu}_\varepsilon f(x)
 =\int_{|x-y|>\varepsilon}K(x-y)f(y)\,d\mu(y).$$ For the smooth family, fix one nondecreasing $\varphi_{\mathbb R}\in C^2([0,\infty);[0,\infty))$ such that, for some finite $C_\varphi>0$, $$\label{eq:variation-cutoff}
 \mathbf1_{[4,\infty)}\le\varphi_{\mathbb R}
 \le\mathbf1_{[1/4,\infty)},\qquad
 \mathbf1_{[1/3,3]}\le C_\varphi|\varphi_{\mathbb R}'|.$$ Put $\varphi_\varepsilon(z)=\varphi_{\mathbb R}(|z|^2/\varepsilon^2)$ and $$T^{K,\mu}_{\varphi,\varepsilon}f(x)
 =\int K(x-y)\varphi_\varepsilon(x-y)f(y)\,d\mu(y),$$ where the cutoff kernel is defined to be zero on the diagonal.

<a id="stmt-cor-variation"></a>
<div id="cor:variation" class="corollary">

<a id="eq:hard-variation-consequence"></a>
**Corollary 29** (Consequences of Mas–Tolsa). *Let $d\ge4$ and $2\le n\le d-2$ be integers, and let $\mu$ be an $n$-Ahlfors–David regular Radon measure satisfying <a href="#eq:riesz-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:riesz-bound">[eq:riesz-bound]</a>. Fix $\rho>2$ and a real odd $C^2$ convolution kernel $K$ satisfying <a href="#eq:variation-kernel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:variation-kernel">[eq:variation-kernel]</a>. For every real $f\in L^2(\mu)$, $$\label{eq:hard-variation-consequence}
 \bigl\|V_\rho((T^{K,\mu}_\varepsilon f)_{\varepsilon>0})\bigr\|_{L^2(\mu)}
 \le C_2\|f\|_{L^2(\mu)}.$$ The principal value $$T^{K,\mu}f(x)=\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow0}T^{K,\mu}_\varepsilon f(x)$$ exists finitely for $\mu$-almost every $x$, belongs to $L^2(\mu)$, and $$\|T^{K,\mu}_\varepsilon f-T^{K,\mu}f\|_{L^2(\mu)}
 \longrightarrow0\qquad(\varepsilon\downarrow0).$$ The vector Riesz family $R_{\mu,\varepsilon}f$ satisfies the analogous hard-variation bound and has an almost-everywhere principal-value limit, with strong convergence in $L^2(\mu;\mathbb R^d)$. The exceptional null set may depend on $K$ and $f$. These are strong-convergence assertions on each input; no operator-norm convergence is asserted.*

*For the fixed profile <a href="#eq:variation-cutoff" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:variation-cutoff">[eq:variation-cutoff]</a>, every real $f\in L^p(\mu)$ with $1<p<\infty$ satisfies <a id="eq:smooth-variation-consequence"></a>$$\label{eq:smooth-variation-consequence}
 \bigl\|V_\rho((T^{K,\mu}_{\varphi,\varepsilon}f)_{\varepsilon>0})
 \bigr\|_{L^p(\mu)}
 \le C_p\|f\|_{L^p(\mu)}.$$ For every real $f\in L^1(\mu)$ and $\lambda>0$, $$\mu\left\{x:
 V_\rho((T^{K,\mu}_{\varphi,\varepsilon}f)_{\varepsilon>0})(x)
 >\lambda\right\}
 \le \frac{C_1}{\lambda}\|f\|_{L^1(\mu)}.$$ The constants may depend on the dimensions, the fixed $\rho$, the kernel bounds, the uniform-rectifiability data, and, for the smooth estimates, the chosen profile and $p$ when relevant.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-variation"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-variation</summary>

*Proof.* If $D_E=0$, the support is empty or a singleton. All the truncated operators above vanish $\mu$-almost everywhere, so the assertions are immediate. Suppose $D_E>0$. Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">3</a> gives uniform rectifiability, with constants $\theta,M$ and admissible radii when the support is bounded. For unbounded support, the forward clauses $(a)\Rightarrow(c)$ and $(a)\Rightarrow(b)$ of Mas–Tolsa’s Theorem 2.3 give, respectively, the hard $L^2$ and smooth $L^p$/weak-$(1,1)$ estimates; their Definition 2.1 is precisely the fixed cutoff above, and their Theorem 1.3 includes the vector Riesz family (Mas and Tolsa 2014) <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[MasTolsa2014Variation](#ref-MasTolsa2014Variation)]. Their range $1\le n<d$ contains the present one. We reduce bounded support to this unbounded setting.

Suppose $D=D_E<\infty$, and fix $a\in E$. Since $n<d$, choose an affine $n$-plane $P$ with $\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(a,P)=4D$, and set $$\sigma=\mu+\mathcal H^n\!\restriction P.$$ For $x\in E$, the diameter bound gives $3D\le\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(x,P)\le5D$. Thus $E$ and $P$ are disjoint and $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\sigma=E\cup P$ is unbounded. Lemma <a href="#lem:global-growth" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:global-growth">4</a> and the Euclidean plane-volume bound give $$\sigma(B(z,r))\le(9^dC_{\rm AD}+\omega_n)r^n
 \qquad(z\in\mathbb R^d,\ r>0),$$ where $\omega_n$ is the volume of the unit ball in $\mathbb R^n$.

We verify lower growth and Lipschitz pieces at every support center and radius. For centers on $P$, the plane supplies both properties at every radius. For $x\in E$ and $r\le D$, use the corresponding properties of $\mu$. If $D<r<12D$, the ball $B(x,D/2)$ lies in $B(x,r)$ and has mass at least $$C_{\rm AD}^{-1}(D/2)^n\ge C_{\rm AD}^{-1}24^{-n}r^n.$$ The uniform-rectifiability map at scale $D/2$ captures at least $\theta(D/2)^n\ge\theta24^{-n}r^n$ in the same ball. Composing that map with $u\mapsto Du/(2r)$ gives a map on $B_{\mathbb R^n}(0,r)$ with the same image and Lipschitz constant at most $M$. If $r\ge12D$, the disk in $P$ centered at $\pi_Px$ with radius $r/2$ lies in $B(x,r)$, since every point $z$ of that disk satisfies $$|z-x|\le |z-\pi_Px|+\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(x,P)
 <r/2+5D\le11r/12.$$ This disk has $\sigma$-mass at least $\omega_n2^{-n}r^n$ and is the image of $B_{\mathbb R^n}(0,r)$ under a $1/2$-Lipschitz affine map. These estimates prove that $\sigma$ is globally $n$-AD regular and uniformly $n$-rectifiable, with constants controlled by the original dimensions, AD data, $\theta$, and $M$.

For any input $f$ in one of the stated $L^p(\mu)$ spaces, extend it by zero on $P$ to obtain $\widetilde f$. Disjointness gives $\|\widetilde f\|_{L^p(\sigma)}=\|f\|_{L^p(\mu)}$. For every positive truncation and every $x\in E$, $$T^{K,\sigma}_\varepsilon\widetilde f(x)
   =T^{K,\mu}_\varepsilon f(x),\qquad
 T^{K,\sigma}_{\varphi,\varepsilon}\widetilde f(x)
   =T^{K,\mu}_{\varphi,\varepsilon}f(x).$$ The vector Riesz truncations have the same identity. Apply the forward estimates to $\sigma$ and restrict the output to $E$. Since $\sigma|_E=\mu$, the identities transfer both the strong bounds and the weak-$(1,1)$ bound to $\mu$, with the required constant dependence.

In either diameter regime, for a real $f\in L^2(\mu)$, Lemma <a href="#lem:global-growth" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:global-growth">4</a> and the size bound in <a href="#eq:variation-kernel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:variation-kernel">[eq:variation-kernel]</a> give $$\int_{|x-y|>\varepsilon}|K(x-y)|^2\,d\mu(y)
 \le C\varepsilon^{-n}.$$ Consequently the displayed hard integrals converge absolutely for every $x$ and tend to zero as $\varepsilon\to\infty$. Approximate $f$ in $L^2$ by compactly supported $L^1\cap L^2$ inputs $f_j$. The same kernel estimate gives pointwise convergence of each positive truncation. Taking the supremum over finite sequences gives $$V_\rho((T^{K,\mu}_\varepsilon f)_{\varepsilon>0})
 \le\liminf_{j\to\infty}
 V_\rho((T^{K,\mu}_\varepsilon f_j)_{\varepsilon>0}),$$ so Fatou’s lemma transfers the cited variation bound to the actual integrals for $f$. For the smooth formulas, the analogous exterior $L^{p'}(\mu)$ kernel estimate, with $p'=p/(p-1)>1$, and Hölder’s inequality give the same identification for $L^p$ inputs. For $L^1$ inputs the cutoff kernel is bounded at each fixed positive scale.

Let $V=V_\rho((T^{K,\mu}_\varepsilon f)_{\varepsilon>0})$. By <a href="#eq:hard-variation-consequence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hard-variation-consequence">[eq:hard-variation-consequence]</a>, $V$ is finite almost everywhere. Finite variation forces the truncations to be Cauchy as $\varepsilon\downarrow0$: otherwise a decreasing sequence with infinitely many jumps bounded below would make $V$ infinite. This proves existence of the finite principal value. The definition gives $|T^{K,\mu}_\varepsilon f-T^{K,\mu}_\delta f|\le V$ for every $\varepsilon,\delta>0$. Sending $\delta\downarrow0$ gives $|T^{K,\mu}_\varepsilon f-T^{K,\mu}f|\le V$, while sending $\delta\to\infty$ gives $|T^{K,\mu}_\varepsilon f|\le V$. Thus the limit belongs to $L^2(\mu)$, and dominated convergence with the majorant $V^2\in L^1(\mu)$ proves strong $L^2$ convergence. The same argument with Euclidean norms applies to the vector Riesz family. ◻

</details>

<a id="app:geometric-conventions"></a>
### Conventions for the bilateral criterion

We justify the precise version of Theorem <a href="#thm:bwgl" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:bwgl">28</a>, including positive image mass and bounded supports. The argument first transfers our open-ball packing estimate to the metric coefficient used by Bate, Hyde and Schul, and then turns one of their bi-Lipschitz pieces into a Lipschitz image of the full parameter ball.

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-variation-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-variation</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:bwgl" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:bwgl">28</a>.* The formulation of Bate, Hyde and Schul uses closed balls and maps defined on all of $\mathbb R^n$ (Bate, Hyde, and Schul 2023, Theorem 1.0.4) <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[BateHydeSchul2023](#ref-BateHydeSchul2023)]. If $\overline b_E$ denotes its closed-ball bilateral coefficient, then $$\overline b_E(x,t)\leq2b_E(x,2t).$$ Indeed, both closed-ball suprema at radius $t$ are bounded by the open-ball suprema at radius $2t$ for the same plane. For $R<\mathop{\mathrm{diam}}(E)/2$, enlarging the spatial ball to $B(a,2R)$ and substituting $s=2t$ therefore transfers the Carleson hypothesis of Theorem <a href="#thm:bwgl" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:bwgl">28</a> to closed balls. If $D=\mathop{\mathrm{diam}}(E)<\infty$ and $D/2\leq R<D$, cover $E$ by a dimensionally bounded number of support-centered open balls of radius $D/8$. On each enclosing radius-$D/4$ ball, the open-ball estimate controls the transformed integral for $t<D/8$. For the remaining $D/8\leq t<R$, the integral is at most $(\log8)\mathcal H^n(E)\leq C D^n\leq C2^nR^n$. Thus the closed-ball criterion applies with controlled constants in both diameter regimes. AD regularity has the same open/closed-ball equivalence, by doubling the radius and using the total-mass bound when that doubled radius exceeds a finite diameter.

We next pass from Euclidean bilateral flatness to the metric coefficient used in that source’s corona decomposition. Its coefficient $\xi_E(a,t)$ (Bate, Hyde, and Schul 2023, Definition 3.1.3) <a id="pm-cite-29"></a>[[BateHydeSchul2023](#ref-BateHydeSchul2023)] minimizes, over maps from $E\cap\overline B(a,t)$ to closed radius-$t$ balls in $n$-dimensional normed spaces, the sum of two quantities: the largest pairwise distance distortion divided by $t$, and the largest distance from a target-ball point to the image divided by $t$. We claim $$\xi_E(a,t)\leq36\overline b_E(a,2t).$$ Choose an almost-fitting affine plane $L$ whose summed directed error at radius $2t$ is at most $2\lambda t$, with $0<\lambda<1/12$. Orthogonal projection, followed by translation, gives $f(x)=\pi_L(x)-\pi_L(a)$ in the Euclidean closed radius-$t$ ball in the direction of $L$. Its distance distortion is at most $4\lambda t$. For a target vector $v$ with $|v|\leq t$, the point $y=\pi_L(a)+(1-6\lambda)v$ satisfies $|y-a|\leq(1-4\lambda)t$. The bilateral estimate supplies $z\in E$ with $|z-y|\leq2\lambda t$, so $z\in\overline B(a,t)$ and $|f(z)-v|\leq8\lambda t$. Thus $\xi_E(a,t)\leq12\lambda$. Letting $\lambda$ decrease to the infimum proves the claim when that infimum is below $1/12$. Otherwise the constant map gives $\xi_E(a,t)\leq3\leq36\overline b_E(a,2t)$. The same doubling of scales and finite-diameter coarse-scale bound proved above now transfer the Carleson estimate to $\xi_E$. By (Bate, Hyde, and Schul 2023, Theorem B, implication $(3)\Rightarrow(5)$) <a id="pm-cite-30"></a>[[BateHydeSchul2023](#ref-BateHydeSchul2023)], $E$ therefore has the corona decomposition by normed spaces required in that source’s positive-mass construction, with controlled constants.

This corona decomposition supplies the bi-Lipschitz pieces in (Bate, Hyde, and Schul 2023, Proposition 9.0.2 and Lemma 9.0.4) <a id="pm-cite-31"></a>[[BateHydeSchul2023](#ref-BateHydeSchul2023)]. Choosing the omitted mass below half the lower AD bound gives a piece $F\subset E\cap\overline B(a,s)$ with $\mathcal H^n(F)\geq c s^n>0$ and a uniformly $K$-bi-Lipschitz map $\phi:F\to\mathbb R^n$. To see directly that a full parameter-ball image suffices, fix $p\in F$ and put $V=(\phi(F)-\phi(p))/(4K)$. Then $V\subset B^n(0,s)$, and $h(u)=\phi^{-1}(4Ku+\phi(p))$ on $V$ is $4K^2$-Lipschitz. Coordinatewise Lipschitz extension gives a map on all of $\mathbb R^n$ with constant at most $4\sqrt d K^2$ and image containing $F$. Choose the preceding bi-Lipschitz piece at $s=r/2$ and restrict this map to the open ball $B^n(0,r)$. Its image contains $F$, while $\overline B(a,r/2)\subset B(a,r)$; the mass bound loses only the factor $2^{-n}$. This proves precisely the open-ball formulation of Theorem <a href="#thm:bwgl" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:bwgl">28</a>. ◻

</details>

  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "The second limiting argument retains the size of the incoming error. Its affine conclusion supplies the improvement needed to propagate flatness. The independent weighted-rigidity lemma also identifies the density in the flat limiting measure.",
      "figure_id": "fig:two-limits",
      "pdf_page": 10,
      "source_evidence": [

          "anchor": "fig:two-limits",
          "exact_quote": "The second limiting argument retains the size of the incoming\nerror. Its affine conclusion supplies the improvement needed to\npropagate flatness. The independent weighted-rigidity lemma also\nidentifies the density in the flat limiting measu",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"

      "witness_description": "A flowchart containing six rectangular boxes arranged in two rows connected by directed arrows:\n- Top row (left to right):\n  1. Box \"Small excess and oscillation\" pointing right to:\n  2. Box \"Flat limiting measure\" pointing right to:\n  3. Box \"Heights divided by the excess scale\"\n- Vertical connector:\n  - Directed arrow pointing downwards from \"Heights divided by the excess scale\" to \"Convergent moments and fractional equation\"\n- Bottom row (right to left):\n  4. Box \"Convergent moments and fractional equation\" pointing left to:\n  5. Box \"Affine height limit\" pointing left to:\n  6. Box \"Relative excess improvement\""
